Summary

Lena is struggling to run a vineyard on her own while her out-of-touch fiancé Jack continues to make matters worse. Making her parents happy was one thing, but throwing her life away just to please them was another. Lena eventually hires a foreman, Kara, to help share some of the workload that is cultivating and harvesting grapes. The infatuation sets in almost immediately. How long can Lena deny her feelings? Or will her dreams of producing her own wine come to a sour and inevitable halt? The better question: How much more loneliness would she be risking either way?

✓♥︎

The Cellars at Marble Falls

by AloneButNeverLonely

    The Bitter White, The Sweet Red

    Chapter Notes

    Very loosely based on Shamim Sarif's "Polarized". It's a bit of that one episode of Leverage, as well. Maybe even My Life is Murder. There's no homophobia, no religious trauma... Lots of angst, longing, etc.

    Chapter One: The Bitter White, The Sweet Red

     

    The riesling was a bit too dry...

    Lena sighed, palm to forehead at her wine bar whereupon her elbow was propped. She scratched some notes into the small leather tome beside the three tasting glasses she’d set out that afternoon— There was the appropriately aromatic yet disappointingly dry Riesling, the Pinot Noir, and last but not least, the Merlot. Tasting the dessert wine that was their very first merlot would only spoil her sophisticated palate in terms of sweetness, but it seemed that her decision to begin with the riesling had also affected her high hopes of turning a profit sometime in the near future.

    It seemed that they had begun badly.

    Lena Luthor was a proud vintner and heiress to a vast commercial real-estate empire, but much to her parent’s dismay, all she wanted to do was make wine. To her, it was the perfect blend of art and science. Meeting such an undertaking with both skill and experience should have made her the envy of all wineries here in the Texas Hill Country, yet plowing her way through a veritable field of peers who looked down upon her simply because of her gender was infuriating. Men were not known to possess any more tastebuds than women did, yet here she was, a mere stewardess inside the castle of her own vineyard.

    Here in the maple tree littered town of Marble Falls, Shade Tree Cellars did appear to most as “castle-like” but was actually the grandest of villas to sit upon fifty acres of grapevine fields. Red clay tiles donned every rooftop, including its impressive, spiraled belltower whose great copper-tin bell rang once upon her arrival, and then never again. The place was old, and falling apart faster than she could put it back together, but at least it was hers. Or, at least she dreamed that it would be one day. Her parents had secured it for her through her father’s business connections, and with it (as well as its accompanying winery) came stipulations, of course. She was to turn a profit in under three years or lose it all. An enormous feat in itself, wherein most vineyards typically go out of business within the first two. The villa’s centerpiece was a sprawling, hedge-lined courtyard that smacked of a maze, but the lamp-lit patio in its middle was the perfect place to entertain patrons and guests of all kinds. Complete with a restaurant quality kitchen, expansive underground network of cellars and more acreage of actual vineyard than she could shake a stick at, Lena was poised to finally make a name for herself in the world of vino. She just needed the proper staff to run such a place. Thankfully, decisions as such were still her area of management.

    Her fiancé Jack was an impatient man. A self-proclaimed oenologist and ever persistent beggar for her affections, Jack Spheer was just as kind as he was eager to sell “his” wine. He was also a condition of Lena’s operating Shade Tree Cellars in the hopes that she might one day own it… She must agree to marry the man.

    So, agree she had.

    Arranged marriages were unfortunately —where her parents were concerned, anyway— not so much a thing of the past. She was to allow Jack to produce his prize-winning wines at the facility and to keep the vineyard from running itself into the ground all at the same time. Everyone besides her seemed to think that this was a relative thing of ease; that they had done her some sort of favor or had set her up for success. There were only two problems with that, and they both, unsurprisingly enough, involved her betrothed.

    Jack made less than spectacular wine.

    …And she did not love him.

    Not in the same way that he had professed to love her, that is. They had known each other since college. He partied. She studied. But as it happened, there were never two more opposite people glued together by a singular passion: Winemaking.

    Lena tasted the pinot and jotted down some more notes. Thankfully, it was an improvement upon the haughty petrol smell of the white riesling.

    The red wine’s raspberry-like notes and dull but sweet aftertaste would pair well with a dish of roast duck… If they could only manage to hire some kitchen staff, of course. It was the merlot, to her great relief, that won out amongst the three with its vibrant plum flavor and dash of bitter dark chocolate. Oaked at just the right time, Lena could see herself sitting back with a glass of Jack’s dark blue merlot after a long day of crunching numbers. It would easily fetch a profit amidst their very first vintage, which was what they needed now more than ever. The winery was so far in the red that Lena was fearful of her father showing back up just to yank her dream out from underneath her like a pulled rug. She’d warned Jack not to pick the grapes so early in the season —the effect of which resulting in the almost face-scrunching tartness of their regrettable riesling— but he had been eager to start bottling and selling as soon as possible.

    The merlot could be bottled and sold, she decided, but the other two wines would need some extra care. There was all of this while they were deep in the throes of their second harvest, and the fact that they had a severe lack of morale amongst their workers in order to make said harvest.

    Prize-winning… She huffed silently. What a load of—

    “There you are.” A voice said all of a sudden from behind her bar stool.

    Lena startled and shot up from her notebook, flipping it closed before Jack could read her rather unapologetic remarks.

    “Oh, my G—” She pulled her lips into a smile, bouncing a small yet nervous laugh from her bosom, “You frightened me.”

    “Serves you right for living all by yourself in a spooky old castle.”

    Lena shook her head, feeling as though she had corrected him on the subject many times now, “Again, not a castle.”

    “Checking my work, I see.” Jack leaned to thumb a page of her notebook open.

    Lena smacked it closed again and then tucked it against her chest like a cherished bible of sorts, “Just ideas, really.” Her eyes flitted from one feature of their very empty and sad looking restaurant and wine bar to another, “For some menu items to serve with the red.” She lied.

    “It’s good, right?” Jack boasted.

    “Mm, that’s certainly a word for it.”

    “Forget about the restaurant.” He went to kiss her lips but found her cheek instead. “We’re here to make world-class vino, remember?” Jack sighed and then gave a rather puppy-dog tilt of his head, “Come out with me tonight.”

    Lena scratched endearingly at his thick beard, patting him there with a warm declination. “Can’t. I have to follow up with the bottle vendors if we want to start filling crates on time.”

    “You can’t manage just one night in the city?”

    “Jack…”

    “You stay cooped up here all the time. I only worry that you’ll forget what the outside world looks like before too long.”

    “Not tonight.” Lena replied, hugging the leather of her notebook a bit tighter.

    “Then how about dinner at my place? We’ll have a nice, quiet evening—”

    Lena finally went to slide out from beneath him and then made a wide step away from her stool. “I’m just—” She fumbled for the right words, “There’s so, so much to do.” Her hand gestured vaguely around the place, “I actually have a round of interviews to get to—”

    “What, for the foreman’s position?”

    “Mhm.”

    “I thought you said we were going to make the harvest this year without one?”

    There was that word she hated, “we”. Lena shook her head more briskly this time, emphasizing upon their dire need to hire a field lead. They would be lucky to harvest any grapes at all without a foreman overseeing the workers— the ones who had signed on to stay for another year either out of sheer loyalty or desperation. Lena couldn’t be sure which.

    “We need a foreman.” Lena shrugged as she continued backpedaling towards the wine bar’s exit.

    “Alright, then.”

    As if she needed his permission.

    Lena scoffed inwardly with the thought and then put her back into the door, making her escape with some haste. Truthfully, Jack wasn’t all that bad. Her parents simply wanted to see her married; to do as she must where a woman of stature was concerned. She was in her thirties with no other prospects as far as a husband, or a future, so Lillian and Lionel Luthor had done what they always did. They stepped in. It was too much of a thing for Lena to stand up to them. It had been that way for her entire life. Her stepmother was callous and unfeeling even on a good day, but her father seemed to have a sliver of respect for her more independent nature. As long as there was money to be made. Their collective and benign emotional neglect had torn a hole, one that she desperately (and almost pathologically) needed to fill. The truth, however, always lay at the back of her mind. A constant reminder of what would undoubtedly happen.

    She would marry Jack and somehow turn a profit at her vineyard all in the same breath, and then her parents would sign over ownership to him, and not her. She knew the ultimate betrayal was already in the works, so why was she going along with it all still? Lena would do all the work while Jack raked in the accolades, all while undoubtedly dodging the discussion of children and heirs-to-be.

    It made her stomach turn.

    Jack had been her friend before anything else, but Lena felt there was no way to salvage what lay ahead of her.

    If she chose to forge her own path, she would surely find herself alone and yearning for the life she had thrown away. Time and time again she ended up regretting each of her decisions to defy her parent's wishes… She’d learned from her past mistakes by now. Though, wouldn’t she still regret bowing to them anyway like a “good” daughter would? There was simply no middle ground to it.

    Everything was hers —or so her parents were chagrined to remind her— but in reality, she would end up with nothing but callused hands and a lonely heart.  

     

     

    “And? What went wrong?”

    Lena grumbled incoherently around the mouth of her wine glass as she and her best friend strolled from one corner of the herring bone tiled room to another. The wine barrel cellar was a viewing room of sorts, filled wall to wall with large oak casks marked with their premier vintage. The previous year’s harvest (now blended and oaked in front of them) still wreaked havoc upon her mind. The stress, the broken machinery, the loss of workers… How they were able to cultivate a second growing season had been nothing short of a miracle, to say the least, but at this rate the grapes would hang upon their vines and overripen themselves into a sickly-sweet oblivion.

    “They were all just…” Lena hummed in search of the right word, “Absolutely dreadful.”

    Sam hacked a laugh at that as she ran her finger over the iron rib of a barrel. “Be serious.”

    “I am!”

    “You really didn’t like any of them?”

    Lena shook her head. “Over half lied on their resumes. Abundantly, too.” She grumbled again, “And the ones who were qualified were so stuck up it was a wonder they could sit down with whatever they had shoved so far up their—”

    “Okay…” Sam lead Lena out of the barrel room and into the wide atrium at the winery’s front. A charm of small, reddish finches had found their way inside and sing-songed happily from the beams above. “I’m barely here an hour and you’re already raving. Let’s talk about something else?”

    “Like?”

    “Well, you haven’t asked about your parents for one thing…”

    “Oh, yes, of course, how silly of me—” Lena played along and gave some rather over-the-top bats of her long, dark lashes, “How are dear mother and father?”

    “Cranky as always. Your dad acquired the office park across from the square- you know the one with the awful metal sculpture out front? Somebody took it pretty hard, I guess. Threw themselves right off the roof—”

    “I’m sorry,” Lena gave little waves of her hands, stopping at the large and ornately carved front doors, “…but how is this better than my not being able to hire one single hillbilly to pick some damn grapes?”

    “Hmm.” Sam nodded.

    They stepped outside into the hot august sun just as a beat-up Ford Bronco pulled into one of the gravel lined parking spots. An arm reached out to pull at the outside latch upon the driver’s door, and there emerged a head of flaxen hair that was pulled up into a high pony.

    “Speaking of hillbillies…” Sam muttered as she and Lena watched the person approach. It was a woman with a rolled-up piece of paper in one hand, red and white plaid shirt unbuttoned and tied in a knot around her middle with a thin white tank top underneath. With boots and jeans that fit just right, Lena was slightly in awe at how naturally the woman had skipped up the front steps to meet them, almost like she had done so a hundred times before. Sam huffed a breath of a laugh, whispering just out of earshot, “Actually, I take that back.”

    Lena gave a split-second twist of her head in her best friend’s direction just to catch the stars there in her eyes, but she shook it off as nothing more than a look of surprise. Sam had lived in New York for too long, that much was certain.

    “My apologies,” Lena looked over her shoulder towards the restaurant and wine bar entrance, where the poorly hung “closed” sign in its window hung a bit too crookedly for her liking, “The bar’s still not staffed—”

    “That’s, um- Well… here.” The woman stuck the piece of paper in her hand out, “I’m here about a different job, actually.”

    “Oh? Which one?” Lena asked.

    “The foreman’s position.”

    Lena shook her head. “I’m sorry, I’m not taking any more interviews for—”

    “She’ll see you.” Sam interjected with an intense look towards Lena, almost as if she were ready and waiting for her to object. She reached a hand out towards the woman then to shake, “Thanks so much for coming… Er, what was your name?”

    “Kara. Kara Danvers.”

    Sam shook the woman named Kara’s hand and then pointed towards the inside of the winery, “There’s a private tasting room through the front doors and down the long hall to the left. You can’t miss it. Lena here will catch up with you in, say, five minutes?”

    “Sure, great. Thanks.”

    Lena went to call out after the eager blonde but found her words trailing away with the soft, summer wind, “She doesn’t actually...” Then her breath billowed out with a sigh, “…work here.”

    “Oh, come on. She seems like a good fit!” Sam insisted with a small stamp of her foot.

    “You decided that in what, less than a minute?”

    “Why not?”

    Lena huffed, “Her resume is one sheet,” She unrolled said resume within her hands and then shrugged, “…but at least it’s printed.”

    “Be nice.” Sam urged as she turned Lena slowly by her shoulders to lead her inside. “You need this.”

    “Wonderful. You know how much I love it whenever people who are not, in fact, me tell me what I need.”

    “At least she doesn’t seem like a snob.” Sam tried.

    “I got that much.”

    “And she’s… you know…”

    “Unconventionally attractive?”

    “Not sure if that’s quite the compliment you were aiming for,” Sam admitted with another laugh, “But I was gonna say that she’s definitely not a man… So, she’s already a sound improvement. Right?”

    Lena hung her head, deciding just to get one last interview over with. “Right.”

     

     

    She felt the urge to check her makeup in the mirror, but wasn’t exactly sure why.

    She wasn’t the one who needed to impress here. And yet…

    The moist corner of a paper towel dabbed at the corners of her eyes. A less racoon-ish look would fare far better if she were to sit down and entertain this Kara person over an admittedly thin resume. Her eyeliner now in better shape, Lena made sure to scoop the rolled up piece of paper along with her on her way out of the bathroom. A quick walk down the hall and into the tasting room brought them face-to-face again, but wait— Had the woman’s smile looked quite like that just a few minutes before? Or had the glare from the sun stolen what pure glory it possessed? The way her cheeks rolled buoyantly upward with joy, just a touch of pink to them as they made half-moons of her eyes. She smiled with those too. It seemed like such an effortless thing…

    To smile like that.

    To smile at her like that.

    Lena cleared her throat and then gestured towards a leather swivel chair, its back half a reclaimed oak barrel and almost on-the-nose as far as a theme. “So,” She sat across from Kara, crossing one knee over another, “First, I have to ask… What makes you want to work on a vineyard?”

    “The truth?”

    “I’d prefer it.”

    “Well, I didn’t at first.” Kara replied.

    “Really?” Lena huffed a laugh, turning her eyes away from Kara’s resume. “May I ask why?”

    “I dunno. Seemed a little out of reach, I guess. I grew up on a farm, so I’m at least good with heavy equipment and hard labor.”

    “What made you change your mind?” Lena inquired, smoothing out the fabric of her slacks where it bunched at her knee.

    “The, um,” Kara seemed nervous as she scratched at the back of her neck and then drew her long, golden ponytail over a shoulder, “The job posting said something about accommodations?”

    “I said that’s what changed my mind. But I think I’m well suited for the job anyway, if given half the chance to talk about my work history.”

    Lena brought her eyes down towards Kara’s resume in her lap, which she then realized was anything but “thin”. The amount of transferrable skills Kara claimed to possess was a complete list, scaling the entire page from top to bottom on one side. There was indeed a farm listed as a previous work history, and then some odd forays into line clearing and road crew companies, but she seemed to have lived and worked in and around Marble Falls for the whole of her life, which was wholly unsurprising to Lena. The people here were happy. They liked where they lived. Many of them stayed just like their parents had, as had their grandparents, and their grandparents before them… Generationally owned farms seemed to exist on every parcel of Texas Hill Country. Lena supposed that it was the limestone rich soil. It was what grew such phenomenal grapes, after all.

    “You’ve really done all of this?” Lena asked, “Tractor equipment repair, animal husbandry, plumbing, electrical—”

    “Sure. That’s what I could fit on the page, anyway.”

    “You mean there’s more?”

    “Yeah…”

    Lena smiled. She decided that she liked this Kara Danvers. “You do know that you can print out a second page, and staple it to the first?”

    “Well,” Kara smiled back at her, “That would just be bragging, wouldn’t it?”

    Lena finally laughed at that. The woman sitting across from her seemed to beam another smile at the sound, like she was proud to have been the cause of it. Lena sighed deeply and then gave a comfortable shift of her hips into her chair, swiveling this way and that upon its base before opening her mouth again.

    “You sure you want it?”

    “Wouldn’t have bothered you about it if I didn’t.”

    Lena chewed on the inside of her lip, looking Kara up and down just a few more times for good measure. She couldn’t quite put her finger on it, but the way her eyes took to her so easily was a bit confounding. Kara Danvers was uniquely fitted for the role. Everything about her seemed to be unique, in fact. It was her choice of clothing and her mannerisms, and the subtle yet humble genius that seemed to lay within. Even the way she held her arms when she walked, not to mention the way her mouth moved whenever she spoke… Lena blinked herself out of her idle staring and cleared her throat again.

    “Alright. It’s yours.”

    “Really? Just like that?”

    “Would you prefer I call you back in three to four business days?”

    “No.” Kara replied. She seemed to be a perfect match for Lena’s level of banter as well. Another plus. “I’m curious, though. You must have interviewed a bunch of other people before me. It’s not like there’s any shortage of people needing jobs in this town… I even showed up a day late. So why me?”

    “For starters, you’re the only person I’ve spoken to that hasn’t taken one good look at me and then asked to speak to the vintner.”

    “Is…” Kara puzzled for a moment, “Is that not you?”

    Lena could only smile at that. God, she was adorable. There was something about her that made Lena absolutely ravenous with the thought of becoming her friend— But there would a balance of power apparent in any future friendship they might form, so Lena would have to remember to tread carefully. Or maybe she did just spend too much time on her own.

    I wonder if she has a boyfriend…

    “You can move some of your things in whenever you’re ready.” Lena said, “We’re not a big corporate outfit here but we do have regulatory visits and meetings, so I’ll expect you to be on time and professional when those visits and meetings happen. Other than that, come ready to work on Monday. Wear steel toed boots, please.”

    “Alright.”

    Lena reached out to shake Kara’s hand; an action that always felt just a little out of place whilst in the company of other women, but Kara was apparently just as uniquely charming as she was skilled. She took Lena’s hand with a gentle squeeze, and Lena looked down. “Well worked” was a good term for it… Looking at Kara’s hand while it held the slightly paler complexion of her own seemed to run the few knots in her stomach loose, almost like something there had given a great and awful flutter. Lena turned Kara’s hand a bit more upright while they stood there apart from one another in silence. There were dark, healing remnants of bruises there upon the backs of her knuckles. Lena felt the furrowing of her brow just as soon as Kara took her hand back.

    “I use a punching bag.” Kara explained, “Bit of a hobby. Keeps me active, anyway.” When Lena nodded with a barely audible “ah”, she turned to step out of the room in order to take her leave. “Thank you, Lena. I’ll see you on Monday.”

    “Mhm.” Lena had a hard time swallowing for some reason. “Key’s under the mat- To the foreman’s quarters, I mean. You’ll see that its fully furnished.”

    “Sounds good.”

    There was that strange feeling in the pit of her stomach again. It had leapt around like a punch-drunk butterfly at the mere sound of her own name. It was the oddest sensation, really… But not completely unfamiliar. There had been a girl from her youth with whom she’d grown close. The greatest of friendships she had ever known— soured by her foolish and unrestrained heart. Together they had burned hot and fast only to be extinguished with the same level of ferocity. It was over just as soon as it had begun, yet, she still wasn’t quite sure what it was to begin with. Those first quiet rumblings of desire… how they shook her to her bones. Nevertheless, Lena had never felt the same repeated with anyone else since. No one had even come close.

    Not until now.

     

     

    The grapes upon this vine were smaller.

    The wood was old, and thick in its strong rooted center stalk. Old grapevines tended to be put out of their misery and pulled free from the earth to spare the soil what nutrients it had left for younger and more flourishing vines, but with age came more concentrated flavors. Lena saw their value. They were hers to cultivate, stuck all the way out here upon the far north end of the vineyard. The view wasn’t bad up on this little hill, either. The foreman’s quarters were just a stone’s throw away.

    Lena had taken her time this morning compared to every other morning she spent with her vines. She would often make it a quick task at the very least, considering the many acres of other vines she should have been tending. They were the ones that had made the wine they were to bottle and sell, after all. Her own little rows of small and plenty had made just enough to fill a forklift’s pallet. Bottled and left unlabeled on her own time, they were a firmly kept secret from that of her fiancé. Lena thought that perhaps he’d be amused at her miniscule undertaking. Either that, or he’d feel a stab of jealousy somehow. Whatever the case, she was too afraid to come clean now. Especially since she’d sequestered one of their facility’s producing vats for her own blend. It had sat in the environmentally controlled vat room along with the others like it to ferment her own grapes instead of the vineyard’s stock. Moving it all from there to hardy, wooden barrels to age had been a simple task in itself; she’d merely marked which casks were hers with a shallow nick into their wood.

    Besides, why shouldn’t she make her own wine?

    If it was all hers but not hers at the same time, then what would it matter if she produced a dozen or so cases of her own vintage?

    Thoughts and worries aside, she continued to prune with the every once in a while glance towards the foreman’s quarters. Kara was lugging one box or suitcase at a time through the red wood of its back door. Knowing that they would greet one another again come Monday morning wasn’t good enough, for whatever reason. Quite frankly, Lena was enamored. Nothing —or no one— so interesting had happened to Shade Tree Cellars in nearly all the time Lena had spent there.

    I guess she’s not afraid of burning if she wears shirts without sleeves…

    The tactile feeling of the fuzzy, invisible hairs upon each leaf of her vines were almost numb to her now. She hardly noticed them at all. If grapevines should happen to possess thorns, one could prick her during a moment like this and she would be none the wiser. The dying yellow of old foliage fell away with gentle sweeps of her hands as she continued to watch the back door of Kara’s smallish, stucco house— but after a while, Lena noticed that the woman herself hadn’t traipsed back to it in some time. Lena leaned upwards onto her toes to see over the trellis of her vines… Kara’s very masculine choice of transportation was still there.

    “Hi.” A voice said far to her left.

    Lena dropped her pruning shears once she startled and put her hand to her heart with a breathy laugh. “Hi…” She said back.

    A flush of warmth quickly raced to her cheeks, and not just because of the summer heat. Kara went to pick up the shears before Lena could think to do so herself.

    Handsome, charming and now chivalrous.

    All she needs is a suit of armor, perhaps a sword—

    Lena abruptly pinched the bridge of her nose in order to wake herself from yet another daydream. She smiled and eventually took the shears back when offered.

    “Sweet…” Kara said.

    Lena blinked, “I beg your pardon?”

    “These grapes here. These will be sweeter than anything else you’ve got out there.”

    “Did I happen to miss ‘viticulture’ somewhere on your long list of applicable skills yesterday?” Lena teased.

    “Grew up on a farm, remember? We had the occasional grapevines here and there.”

    “Right.”

    “When they get old like this, they just taste better for some reason.”

    Lena smiled. “I agree.”

    “I, um, wanted to ask about your visitor’s policy- Since I forgot to whenever we spoke yesterday.”

    “Visitors, like friends?”

    “I have those, yes.” Kara chuckled and then gave a cautious yet gentle wave between them, “Good ones… don’t worry.”

    “Well, you’re far enough away from the villa so that if you do happen to make a lot of noise, nobody’s going to hear it, really. You can have friends over after your work is done, but I’m telling you, there won’t be much time left for any visiting at the end of the day. That and you wouldn’t have the energy to—”

    “Don’t be so sure.” Kara quipped almost instinctively. “Energy, I have.”

    Lena felt that godawful flutter deep in her stomach again. Wait, were her knees actually juddering in place?

    Stop it.

    Change the subject.

    Lena shrugged with as much nonchalance as she could muster and then folded her arms across her chest. A moment later, the wide brim of her straw hat blew in the wind, and she went to catch it. Playing with the string that drooped low beneath her chin after that seemed like the obvious choice whenever it came to her fidgeting hands. Since when did she fidget?

    “So… Tell me a little about your farm, Kara.”

    Kara’s gentle smile then turned down with a frown. “We grew prickly pear.” She tried her best then to encourage another smile upon her face, but it fell quickly just like the first. “Cactus.”

    “Grew, as in past tense?”

    “Mm.” Kara hummed in response. It seemed that Lena had touched a nerve. She wanted to remove them both from it as quickly as possible, but wherever on earth to turn the conversation next? Kara, thankfully, beat her to the punch. “I also wanted to thank you again- for the job.”

    “You were the best candidate. Better late than never, I suppose.”

    “I guess so. Thank you anyway.” Kara said as she ground one of the heels of her boots into the fertile soil before turning away. Lena was saddened for some reason to see her leave, but Kara quickly turned back around as if she had been stricken with another thought. “Speaking of friends… You mentioned that you’re still meaning to hire some kitchen staff?”

    “Um,” Lena jawed around the question for a moment, “I did, yes. Half the winery’s trouble is the lack of business, but that’s just between you and me.”

    “Don’t worry…” Kara finally smiled, gallant once again, “I can take care of that, too.”

    Chapter End Notes

    Thanks for reading <3

    Heroes in Disguise

    Chapter Summary

    Lena finds promise in two newcomers.

    Chapter Notes

    Decided to keep the momentum. I haven't actually written a story in full Lena POV, so I'd like to do that here. I hope you like it.

    Chapter Two: Heroes in Disguise

     

    Some of the columns just weren’t adding up.

    Maybe she’d applied the wrong function somewhere or had applied the incorrect value to a certain cell. Numbers were her forte, but this didn’t make any sense.

    Lena tapped the return key on her laptop with a vengeance, growing more and more frustrated with the same end result. Why did she even bother? She could do the math in her head. But that was the thing of it… Surely enough, she had the sneaking suspicion of what might be the cause. It sat at the back of her mind, gnawing to its heart’s content.

    Great.

    Just what this place needs—

    The sounds of choking engine sputters made it hard to concentrate on top of everything else. Near to her boiling point, Lena pushed her office chair out from underneath herself with a vexed harrumph and then went to the window. Since when was hand-picking grapes so noisy? The more she stared through the sun’s harsh rays, the more she realized that the source of the noise was nowhere to be found. It was a chugging sort of racket, rolling over and over again as if a large engine block was simply too tired to start.

    -agh gagh gagh gagh-

    -karrump-

    -agh gagh gagh ga—

    “Alright, that’s enough.” Lena grumbled on the way out of her ground floor office.

    She took one turn at a time towards the noise with swift precision. Heels had seemed the appropriate choice for that day, since she was still elbow deep in the numbers which perplexed her so. Their speedy, stilettoed -tack tack tack- noise echoed into the tall rafters of the villa’s atrium, pausing only long enough for her to redirect her exasperation once again. It was hot outside. What a surprise. She went right around the courtyard, and forward through the outer privet hedges. Shit… Lena blustered. Don’t get lost in here again.

    There came the noise yet again, this time with a continued arrogance as it struggled to maintain working order. Someone would definitely have to explain—

    “Oh.” Lena came to a full stop all of a sudden in the unkempt grass, “It’s you…”

    Kara accidentally bumped the back of her head against the underside of a faded red engine cover. She held what looked like a throttle switch open for the antique block to run, but having caught Lena’s approach out from the corner of her eye, she’d let it go. It rattled to a stop with an unhappy -clunk- sound.

    “Sorry…”  Kara apologized, rubbing the back of her head with a slight wince.

    Lena’s frustration about the irritating engine clamor was suddenly a thing of the past. Instead, a feeling much like admiration seemed to sit squarely in its place. Kara had apparently fixed the very old harvester tractor and was covered with dust and whatever else up to her elbows. She wiped her arms and hands clean with a red shop cloth and then tucked it into her back pocket to wave Lena over.

    “Figured I’d poke around a few of these to see which were worth salvaging.”

    “Your diagnosis?” Lena crossed her arms as she felt a corner of her mouth turn up into a smile. Her painfully obvious dimple had been on full display for the past few days… Every time Kara was near, that is.

    “She just needed some love.” Kara replied.

    Did she now?

    The admittedly attractive foreman closed the tractor’s long hood then with a small -creak- and then a quiet but inevitable -slam-.

    “Do you mean to say that we can use it now?” Lena asked.

    “Yeah, why not? I had a spare International 354 filter lying around… The rest are pretty much ready to work.” Kara gestured towards the short row of others: the front loader, the rock picker, the mower…

    “You fixed the rest of them…” Lena huffed a surprised breath, “You fixed the rest of them?!”

    “Well, to be fair, they weren’t exactly broken—”

    Lena went to eye the equipment a bit closer, but the soft ground beneath her feet took up one of her stilettos like a well laid trap. It snapped off and left her stumbling forward, right into Kara’s outstretched arms.

    “Woah.” Kara looked down between them, “You alright?”

    “I think so…” Lena nodded with a small hop. She looked around for a moment, unsure if whether she could hop some more across the slightly rocky landscape with such tender feet or not. She reached down and plucked the broken shoe from her foot, noticing the way Kara’s eyes seemed to widen at the sight of its pristine red lacquer bottom.

    “Do you want me to—”

    “Would you mind?”

    Kara shook her head in agreement and then swung an arm low, scooping Lena up by the backs of her knees. Holding Lena within the space of her arms seemed like such an easy thing to do. The taut stretch of muscle that were her biceps glistened there in the sun, slightly bulged at their middles. Lena’s mouth suddenly ran dry. She was of course caught off guard with how strong Kara was. With one arm looped around the blonde’s shoulders, Lena waited with bated breath until they were flat upon the courtyard brick once again.

    …But the whole way there felt like a lifetime.

    It was the steady flow of breaths in and out from Kara’s nose… The thunderous feeling that rose up from the earth with each step… The way her hands gripped firmly at the bend of Lena’s legs as well as the backs of her ribs…

    Kara had eventually placed her down with a kind of gracefulness befitting royalty. She’d made sure that Lena could at least meet the brick with her feet first before leaving her to stand on her own, like bending down to draw water patiently from a well.

    “Thank you.” Lena felt herself blush.

    “What was your policy about steel toed boots, again?” Kara asked in jest.

    “I’ve been stuck in the office all day!”

    It felt natural, their back and forth banter. Like most things which included Kara Danvers, it just seemed so effortless. Lena suddenly remembered that she’d left her broken shoe back at the tractors and shot an arm out towards it in dismay.

    “Oh, I left—”

    Kara waved her off in reply, “Don’t worry. I’ll take care of it.”

    “I can’t believe you fixed all that equipment—” Lena bounced a laugh from her chest then, “Actually- yes, I can. At any rate, thank you.”

    “You’re welcome.”

    Lena bent to remove her other shoe from her foot, feeling just a shade awkward for standing at such an imbalance. The brick was warm beneath her bare feet and she was glad to feel so rooted again amongst nature. Lena loved to be outside. The very dear memories she had of running barefoot through blanketed fields of wild clover were always close at hand. She looked upon Kara with another small sigh through her nose. What might have Kara’s childhood looked like back on her farm? Perhaps she hadn't been barefooted, though, what with all of the prickly pear cactus about.

    “How are you liking it so far?” Lena asked, “The work?”

    Kara placed her hands upon her hips with a small twist towards the acreage of vineyard that laid beyond. “It’s satisfying- getting things done, moving things along. The fieldhands like to sing a lot… Makes the day go by faster.”

    “And your living quarters?”

    “Fine, fine.”

    “Good.” Lena took a deep breath then, unsure of where to lead the conversation next. She wasn’t quite finished talking with Kara, that much she knew for sure. Anything to keep her foreman’s absolute sunbeam of a smile pointed in her direction. It was selfish, maybe… But Lena didn’t care. “I’m sure everyone will be pleased to not have to use the scythes anymore. I’ve been on Jack to see about setting some money aside for mowers, and now we have one.”

    “More than one would still be nice.” Kara admitted with a hint of a laugh, “Is that the tall, bearded guy who pops in and out every once in a while?”

    Lena felt a knot form in her throat. It hadn’t occurred to her to speak so directly about Jack until now. She’d been much too preoccupied with, well, literally anything else… Especially things that were Kara-shaped.

    To barrel forward and tell the truth, or to go with the vague and simply refer to him as her business partner? She rolled her lips into her mouth for a second, hastening her decision more towards the truth.

    “My fiancé, yes… He does, every once in a while, grace us with his presence. It’s his wine made here, after all.”

    Kara made a quick effort to clear her throat and then looked down at the tops of her boots. They were rubbed raw in one spot at their toes to reveal the metallic shine of steel. She worked hard; there was evidence of that everywhere Lena laid her eyes upon her. For the most part, Kara seemed unfazed at the admission. Lena thought for a moment… Maybe she was truly too invested. Why on earth couldn’t she pull herself away from this woman? And why did she care so much if she knew whether or not she was engaged?

    “There’s, um, some issue with the crusher that I need to go check out soon.” Kara said, “If that thing decides to go out, then it’s likely you’ll have some stomping to do.”

    “Heaven forbid.” Lena smiled.

    “Have a good rest of your day.”

    “What, in my nice, air-conditioned office? Torture.”

    Kara shook her head with a breathy chuckle and then left for the facility side of the winery. Lena was left to stand there and wallow in her absence, kicking herself for being just this side of obsessed.

    Get a hold of yourself.

    She works for you.

    Lena shook her head.

    …And you’re engaged to be married.

     

     

    Just like that, the week had come and gone.

    Lena stretched her neck back and forth from the comfort of her favorite patio chair. It was the one most easily reached inside the ever-cornering hedge maze, complete with small powder-coated table and propane patio torch. She liked to sit here at the close of each week just to gaze up at the pale blue sky as it washed into an orange sunset. When it turned dark, the stars came out and were simply the brightest Lena had ever seen them. Took her breath away, they did. Most of the time she brought a book or a nice cup of tea, but this evening, she came empty handed. There was far too much on her mind.

    Hiring Kara had been her best and worst decision yet. While she was ever so distracting, she was good for the vineyard. That much was apparent. Everyone liked her and seemed to have no trouble at all following her orders. Out from Kara's mouth, they seemed less like orders and more like “would you do me the great honor of…” for whatever task was needed. She treated people as if they were family. This, Lena surmised, had been the biggest hurdle of all when it came to acclimating to Kara’s gentle rays of kindness. Living in New York for so long had nearly bled her dry whenever it came to social niceties. She found that she was unintentionally rude at times and had been consistently taken aback at how many people offered to hold doors open for her. It wasn’t too long after moving down south that she remembered how unhurried people could be. They got up with the sun and worked hard, and then they went home to their loved ones. Easy. Simple. There wasn’t any shame in that, it was clear to see. They all seemed to look after one another, as well. Small communities were, in fact, just very large families.

    It certainly wasn’t hard to enjoy the quiet, either.

    Ursa Major was on full display tonight where it laid bespangled atop a dark blue blanket. Dry lightning cracked across the sky at a distance, drawing Lena’s eyes to the east with a flash. Summer storms were intense. She had yet to get used to the sirens. But the flashes came so intermittently and from so far away that she doubted the vines upon her vineyard would drink in even a single drop of rain. Though, when it rained… it poured. She was, for now, content just to close her eyes to the wide starry vastness above and to take in the sounds of cricket-laced silence.

    She might have even fallen asleep too, if not for the wafting smell of cooked food. Something roasted and delicious danced upon the subtle wind to tease her senses.

    That smells…

    Lena’s mouth began to water.

    What is that?

    Drawn by such an alluring scent, she was up and out of her patio chair before she even knew it. Around each privet corner she went, through and through until the restaurant and bar side of the winery stared back at her with full lights. There were people inside. Someone was having dinner.

    And Lena was curious.

    She made her entrance a quiet one, though the two chatting heads that bobbed animatedly at one another turned to greet her almost immediately. Kara stood there with another woman who was slightly shorter, and had hair pulled into braids along the scalp of her head on one side. She had kind eyes as well. Dripping in beaded bracelets and possessing a taste for fashion that might make any indie designer jealous, she stepped towards the front door of the restaurant to offer her hand out. Lena shook it with a smile.

    “It’s so nice to meet you, Kara’s told me all about the place—” The woman smiled enthusiastically again, “My name is Nia.”

    “Lena. It’s… nice to meet you as well.” Lena tried emulating a sense of “welcome surprise” instead of “annoyed interruption”. She wasn’t altogether sure if her face got the message or not. She didn’t wish to be rude.

    “Are you hungry?”

    “I mean,” Lena bobbed her own head back and forth at the question, “I am now. Have you cooked?” She gave a few polite sniffs into the air to emphasize upon her interest.

    “For you, yes—!” Nia scooted a chair out from a table and then gestured there with a respectful bow, “Sit, please.”

    “O-kay.”

    As soon as her bouncing braids were through the double doors behind the bar, Lena turned to Kara with a puzzled look upon her brow. “Do you know what’s happening?”

    “Yeah. You’re conducting an interview.”

    “I am?” Lena hacked a laugh.

    “Is that okay…?”

    “I—” She fumbled for a moment, much too pleased to do anything other than oblige the situation at hand, “You know what? Sure.” Lena smiled, feeling the dimple in her cheek grow deeper by the second. Then she sat forward again, “Remind me, though. Interview for…?”

    Kara laughed in reply, “The kitchen staff. Nia’s been a maître d' for as long as I can remember.”

    “Oh, right. Friend of yours, I’m assuming?”

    “More like my sister, actually.”

    “Oh. Well, she seems nice.”

    Before long, Nia returned with a hotel quality silver cloche and serving dish, placing it upon the cloth draped table at its very center. She lit a candle stick right beside to add a bit of ambiance and then fetched two bottles from a matching silver ice bucket nearby.

    “For you, this evening, we have a delightfully crisp white riesling that thrills the tastebuds with hints of apple, or a slightly dry yet sweet pinot noir whose chocolatey atmosphere warms up nicely to any roast dish.”

    Lena couldn’t help but to smile from ear to ear. She gave a narrow, scrupulous squint of her eyes then towards the unlabeled greenish-opaque bottles in each of Nia’s hands, “These wouldn’t happen to be Shade Tree wines, would they?”

    Kara leaned forward from where she stood near the next table, hands clasped behind her back. “I may have bribed a cellarhand…”

    “Of course.” Lena huffed a laugh and then turned back to Nia, “The red, please.”

    “Absolutely!” Nia said as she poured into the glass upon the table with expert precision. Then the cloche came up from the serving dish, “Our Michelin-starred chef would like to introduce to you his signature dish, a savory roast quail on toast. The quail is served atop bar grilled sourdough and a chicken liver and foie gras parfait, and then drizzled with a sherry caramel vinegar. It’s finished nicely with some potato crumble and mustard frills.”

    Lena was stunned.

    “Enjoy.”

    “Oh…” Lena clasped her hands in excitement, “I think I might marry it.”

    The first bite was everything and more— She couldn’t believe the flavors. There hadn’t been a meal like this to grace her tastebuds in a very long time. Lena pulled together every ounce of strength not to break down and cry. A sniffle went through her nose before she finished off the first taste of her quail with a small sip from her wine glass. The pinot was good, but honestly, this dish deserved better.

    “Amazing—” Lena spoke politely from behind her hand, “Just amazing. Would you do me a favor, though?”

    “Yes, anything, of course.”

    “Would you peek behind the bar and grab the bottle that’s tucked away behind some dining room catalogs?” Nia gave a whimsical nod of her head and went to procure the bottle. “And some more glasses, please?” Lena asked.

    The young woman returned with yet another smile upon her face. She was right to be happy. She’d just earned herself a job. “A special vintage, I’m guessing?” Nia asked.

    “Just last year. My own blend.” Lena replied, “Would you be so kind now as to produce the chef who made this?” She cut another quick bite of breast meat and chewed quickly, not wanting to let the dish turn cold.

    “Brainy!” Nia whisked herself quickly into the kitchen once again and returned upon the heels of a mousy looking gentleman. He had a distinguished nose and flat black hair, but his lips seemed to purse with an anxious nature. Nia pushed him closer by the shoulders, encouraging him to present his creation proudly. He finally cleared his throat and then straightened his back in an effort to speak, chin struggling to hold itself high.

    “Barney Fife, ma’am.” He introduced himself, “Is the quail to your liking?” His dark and somewhat beady eyes flitted towards the serving dish in front of her.

    Lena answered straight away, “Barney, when can you start?”

    “Yesterday, ma’am.”

    Lena laughed. The young man seemed to have a deadpan sort of humor about him, and that was something that Lena could appreciate… Not to mention his award-winning culinary skills.

    “Fantastic. I’ll expect the both of you here bright and early Monday morning to go over some menu options. This will have to be the main dish, of course.” Lena quickly reared her head back at the fact that each of them were still standing around her, and she gave some rather flamboyant waves of her arms then for them to sit. “Pull up a chair! Drink! Is there enough food left?”

    Nia gave a happy squeal and skipped into the kitchen a third time. She came back with a row of plates stacked skillfully up the length of her arm and then poured each of them a measure from Lena’s bottle.

    Lena gave a raise of her glass towards the table, “To new beginnings.”

    Each of them rang out with the same heartfelt sentiment, with “Brainy” being the quietest amongst them. He seemed like a gentle soul, reserved and calm throughout any storm. Nia was his perfect foil, as well. Lena could tell that they were an item just by the way they looked at one another. To love someone like that… She wondered if such a thing would ever be possible for herself. True love like theirs seemed like such a rare thing indeed. Lena gave a quiet, satisfied hum into the mouth of her wine glass, pleased with the way it tasted after such careful devotion.

    Nia was the first to remark at its quality. Her beautifully thick eyebrows went up with an ounce of surprise as she turned to Kara, “This is good. You didn’t tell me the wine was this good.” She tongued lightly at her lips and then tasted again, “Strong currant flavor, slightly sweet yet acidic… A cabernet, definitely.” Her heart-shaped eyes then went to Lena, “Did you cask this in a cherry wood?”

    “Wow.” Lena drawled openly.

    “How are you not selling this like crazy?”

    Lena was thrilled with such a warm review. “Well, enjoy it while you have it, because there’s not actually that much to sell. I have maybe twenty cases that passed muster. A lucky first try, if anything.”

    “If there was ever a more modest answer, then I haven’t heard it.” Nia replied, “But you say that like you don’t actually have any intention of selling it… Why?”

    “My fiancé- who is also my business partner- doesn’t exactly know. If you catch my drift.”

    “Ah.”

    “But thank you,” Lena opened her hand towards the table full of food and drink, “…for this.” She went on with a smile, “I imagine this will be the start of something very special. Who knows, you three might just be my saving grace.”

    Chapter End Notes

    Thanks for reading <3

    Chateau Danvers

    Chapter Summary

    Lena fixes a mistake.

    Chapter Notes

    Chapter Three: Chateau Danvers

     

    The sheer, door length drapes of her bedroom windows let in just enough light.

    It was a welcome relief not to wake with the tinny sounds of an electronic alarm coming from her phone each morning. Sunrise was around seven o’clock this time of year, but most of the fieldhands took advantage of the cool early hours by getting out into their sectioned rows much sooner than that. It made the picking easier; to have it mostly done before the sweltering heat waves bore mercilessly down upon them. The harvest would go into the destemmer and crusher after that. From there, the grape must was gravity fed into the underground parts of the facility, all the way down to their allocated vats for fermenting.

    Lena usually helped. If it got her out from behind a desk, then she was all over it. Since Kara (and now by extension Nia and Brainy) knew about her private stash of Cabernet Sauvignon vines, she’d been walking the half-mile distance each morning just to continue their secret tryst— of harvesting grapes that is. It certainly made the days go by faster. Production was at an all-time high.

    Kara was funny. The more time Lena spent in her wonderfully sunshiny company, the more she seemed to forget about everything else.

    Eager to begin the day, Lena stretched her arms above her head with a yawn. Her room at the far end of the villa was old, much like the rest of the place. It was cordoned off by large pedestal archways and two massive oak doors that were quite heavy to either pull or push shut. Many of the wall sconces and candelabras inside were adorned with iron rings, likely all original, even down to their hardened wax drippings. Lena simply adored the tall, dome-shaped ceiling of her bedroom. She felt that it gave her as much breathing room as the outdoors did. Each step echoed as she made her way into the adjoining bathroom with its wall-to-wall tile. The decorative stained glass of her shower door provided a cozy kind of darkness— Not so much that she had to search around blindly for her shampoo bottles, but just enough that it felt like standing beneath the smooth, running falls over the mouth of a cave. She could think freely here. It was safe, and secure… and truly nobody else’s except that of her own.

    This was where she could wash away her anxiety.

    There were no more eyes constantly watching her every move. No more paranoid insisting that everyone could hear what she was thinking, either. The tension she held at the base of her neck slowly began to melt. Deep breaths found their way in and out through her nose with all of the hot water raining down upon her. She knew that she would eventually need to step back out again and face reality once more, and even though Kara was there to make brighter each of her days, it still didn’t seem to be enough. Something was missing. Her life was whole, to be sure, except for one small thing…

    It was a nameless sort of feeling that dug a hole somewhere near the thrumming beat of her heart. She felt that she missed whatever it was, and deeply, too, but therein lied the conundrum of it all. How could you end up missing a thing that you’ve never even had? What Lena desired more than anything in the world, more than making wine, or travelling, or having a nice, quiet place to call home… was softness.

    Of all the things she had, she longed for something intangibly soft. A gentle caress to soothe the soul.

    Whether it be physical or emotional, or in the shape of a friend or loved one, she simply wished to be cherished. Lena felt her hand as it splayed wide over her ribs. She meant to hug herself. It had been so long since she’d been hugged…. She needed it; to be held. Jack was always a bit too oblivious and far too abrupt all at the same time, somehow. The coarse hairs of his beard scratched at her neck whenever he did choose to be affectionate, and his hands… Well, they had an awful habit of wandering. He could never just be still with her. No, she didn’t love him. But then again, she had never really loved anyone. Not in that way, at least. Maybe she just didn’t know how. Maybe a love like that simply didn’t exist inside of her. Maybe she was just incapable.

    …But when she thought of Kara?

    Oh, how all of her twisted up knots seemed to work their way loose again.

    Lena’s hand smoothed down the rest of the way to reach for the inside of her thighs. She felt around there silently in the comfort of her steaming shower, each of her breaths patient and heavy against the dark, foggy glass. Kara’s eyes had lingered much too long in her direction these past few weeks, and their standing beside one another deep in the hillocks of sprawling grapevines had been much too close… Every once in a while, Kara’s hand had grazed ever so accidentally across her own. Gardening gloves or no, Lena had felt it. The way it shot straight to her bones, and proceeded to grow like warmed lantern light deep inside her belly…

    Wait.

    She opened her eyes again.

    Stop.

    A sharp breath went through her nose as she stood straight once more, having slumped comfortably forward with her arm braced against the shower. Lena could feel the thundering of her heart as it fought to move aggressively up the length of her throat. Letting her mind run away with itself seemed just the severe opposite end of keeping it locked away and all to herself. What a fool of herself she would make, lusting after one of her employees.

    “Get a hold of yourself.” She said, “You have a job to do.”

    And do that job she would…

    But maybe with just a little bit of a skip in her step. Besides, what did her small sense of giddiness actually hurt?

    The droning hum that came with the rising sun was loud and clear by the time she made her way outside. Far north of the villa and down the beaten path she went to meet Kara for another day of mindless grape picking— but unlike every morning prior, Kara was nowhere to be found.

    Lena traipsed back through the adjacent plots, tucking her head down each row one at a time in search of anyone that might aid her. “Excuse me—” She called out towards a fieldhand in faded denim overalls, “Have you seen Kara?”

    They replied with a simple shake of their head “no.”

    The more workers Lena asked, the more they seemed to think it odd right along with her. Kara was a great many things, but she was punctual most of all. Lena decided just to turn around and head straight for her foreman’s quarters. She thought maybe there was a good reason for such a sudden absence, thinking it only temporary, but when she saw that the punching bag that normally swung from the back porch rafters was no longer there, her feet came to a clumsy halt in the dirt. Then, Lena began to run.

    Heart in her throat once again, she slowed upon her approach. She didn’t want to seem “overly” concerned about Kara’s mysterious disappearance, but the pit in her stomach sprang forth again once she noticed that the inside of the small house had been cleaned out. It was like Kara had never been there to begin with. Bed made and extra blanket folded respectfully at its foot, Lena sat her hands upon her hips while she spun exasperatedly in place. Kara had gone and taken her things with her, though, it seemed that she had at least left one thing behind.

    There upon the small countertop of the kitchenette sat Lena’s long-lost Louboutin, complete and made whole again with a note beneath its repaired stiletto…

    “For Cinderella.”

    Why had Kara not simply written her name? Had their silent sort of closeness been that much of a secret? Lena suddenly felt like crying.

    Thankfully, the sound of Jack’s Land Rover careening down the main drive was all it took to pull herself back together again. Lena jogged back through Kara’s back door and towards the villa, still not quite sure what to make of that morning so far. She had more questions than anything. Had the work been too much to handle? She thought hard about it. She had warned Kara… But then she remembered that Kara claimed to find the work fulfilling. Had somebody done something to upset her, maybe?

    Lena’s gardening boots absorbed each hurried step as she found her way inside the business wing of the winery. The villa was squared on all sides, sharing westerly living quarters with an east facing storefront. The property was split north by south with the winery’s staging areas and production areas taking up equal space. Cool, arid underground cellars stretched the entire expanse. It was a lot of area to cover in search of Jack, and Lena feared the worst…

    That he had found out.  

    Lena took the long stone steps down to the facility’s vat room where Jack usually stopped first. He did at least seem to understand that the fermenting stage was pivotal in the wine making process, as he made himself available for periodic checks more often than not.

    There he was, staring upward into a handheld hydrometer as always. He doffed a surprised look upon his brow once Lena eventually wheeled to a stop in front of him.

    “What’s the matter?” He asked, seeing her slightly distressed state.

    “Kara’s not said anything to you, has she?” Lena huffed and puffed, trying her best to seem as unbothered as possible.

    “Mm,” Jack hummed, “…who?”

    “The foreman- I’m not sure what’s happened, but the workers don’t seem to have any idea where she is.”

    Jack pulled a hairnet from the slicked-back coif of his black hair and then gave a nod. “Oh.” He replied, “I fired her.”

    Lena felt her heart plunk downward towards her stomach like that of a heavy stone. “You what?” She asked breathily, sweeping some of her own dark hair from her face.

    “Yeah. You know those cabernet vines near the little house out there? She was stealing. Of all the ungrateful—”

    “Jack.”

    “Yes, my love…?” He said as he squinted upwards into his device again, holding it up towards the light.

    “I picked those grapes.”

    She and Kara both had, but that wasn’t the point.

    Jack made a puzzled face once again and looked back at her. “I don’t understand. Those vines aren't supposed to be in production- are they?”

    “What does it matter?”

    “Because they’re our grapes—”

    “No,” Lena could feel the heat of her anger rising, “They’re my grapes, Jack.”

    He frowned then at the strict change in her voice and began to speak softly to her as if she were a child, “You’re upset—”

    “Of course I’m upset!” That was when her voice began to echo off the steel tank surfaces of their many vats. “You can’t just go around firing people without telling me!”

    “I didn’t think that I needed to bother you with it.”

    “You made a mistake.” Lena asserted, but a bit more quietly.

    “Clearly.”

    Lena growled a vexed breath through the hard clench of her teeth and then turned around on a dime. Sometimes her fiancé could be especially dense, but then again, she supposed that it wasn’t actually anyone’s fault… He hadn’t known about those particular vines. She hadn’t exactly told him anything, in fact. Jack didn’t seem to have a problem with giving Lena the latitudes she desired, to work steadily on her own and to not be bothered, but in doing so, she’d only caused herself another problem.

    One that she would mean to fix, and with haste.

     

     

    Was this really the place?

    Lena blinked rapidly at the GPS on the dash of her Mercedes. Sometimes it wasn’t so accurate this far out into the countryside. Maybe she should have made a turn at that last caution light—

    Or perhaps she had ended up right where she was meant to.

    The squarish end of Kara’s Bronco peeked out from around back of the long trailer home. This was indeed the address that she’d left on her personnel file. A wake of greasy-winged buzzards circled overhead as Lena stepped out onto the gravel drive, ominous in their intent as always. There were wide bunches of cacti beneath the crooked frame of a nearby mailbox. It possessed a large dent in its side as if someone had swung a bat hard against it. Lena was familiar with the practice; called “Mailbox baseball”. Boredom seemed to get the better of a teenager’s judgement in places like this, or so she had learned.

    The sounds of yelling voices grew louder as she stepped closer to the trailer’s front. Two steps up the rickety makeshift stairs, and then she was knocking upon the front door. The yelling suddenly stopped. There was some stomping that drew near one moment, and then the squealing hinge of the door as it opened next.

    Kara stared back at Lena with wide, surprised eyes. She had a deep purple bruise that adorned the prominent end of her jaw, and when Lena’s eyes immediately tunneled there, she went to pull her hair across her shoulder in an effort to hide it. Even though it was just a few seconds of stilled silence that transpired between them, Lena’s eyes took themselves further in. She could see the shuffling feet of an older man with an oxygen tank in tow as he struggled to sit down in a fluffy recliner. A woman about Kara’s age stomped right behind him and went out the back door with a -slam- as if she were angry. Kara finally seemed to snap to her senses just as another woman —old enough to be her mother— hurried after the man with a damp cloth to his forehead.

    Kara shut the door upon the trailing end of a cough.

    Lena realized that she had definitely interrupted something, but the faint reminder of Kara’s hasty disappearance rooted her with purpose once again. She was still just a touch upset. Lena straightened her shoulders then and shoved the yellow top of a hardhat towards Kara’s abdomen.

    “Here.” She said.

    Kara raised a curious eyebrow back at her. “What’s this for?”

    “You’ll need it when you come back to work.”

    “I thought you fired me.”

    “I didn’t fire you.” Lena clarified with a narrowing of her eyes, “It was a decision made in error by my—”

    “Fiancé. Right.” Kara’s eyes averted left and then right, landing practically anyplace other than Lena.

    Lena sighed.

    I need you to come back.

    “We need you to come back.”

    I can’t do this without you.

    “We can’t do this harvest without you.”

    Damn the woman’s puppy dog eyes… Kara lifted her gaze and gave a few, pretty bats of her fine golden lashes. “Really?”

    “Mhm.” Lena rolled her lips into her mouth with her answer. She wanted to reach out and wrap her arms around Kara, but alas, they weren’t that friendly despite her deep and somewhat throbbing desire to be. Her eyes went longways past Kara’s shoulder again and towards the front door, “Is everything alright?”

    “Eh,” Kara shrugged, “That’s nothing. Just a regular day at Chateau Danvers.”

    “Okay.”

    “I’ll finish up here and head that way soon.”

    “If you need the rest of the—”

    Kara shook her head quickly, “I don’t.”

     

     

    Another full basket went into the hopper.

    Lena stepped down from the tall ladder of their grape crusher and smiled into the evening wind, proud of taking yet another day under her belt of harvesting what would undoubtedly be a better vintage than the first.

    Now it was time to clean up. Once she saw that all of the grapes had been run through the machine, and that the must which sloped down the other side had gone into its allocated chute, she balled her fist against the red shut-off switch and hit it with a -thunk-. For whichever plot the fieldhands were harvesting each day, they left their baskets stacked upon one another as instructed at the end of each row. Kara would come along to gather them up and stow them away safely inside the facility before long. There was another gleeful skip in Lena’s step as she ran out through the open bay door and into the waning sunlight. She wanted to catch Kara in time to get one last quick chat in before the day was over. It didn’t have to be anything specific. Just something to get the cool and mellifluous vibrations of Kara’s voice sauntering sweetly into her ear.

    There she came down the side of the acre-wide plot. Right on time, just like always. Lena no longer held the desire to admonish her heart for the little leaps it made. Nobody knew the truth of what she felt; It was her secret to keep. It was knowing that, and the fact that nobody could take it away from her, no matter what.

    Kara soon huffed a laugh in her direction, “I’d like to see you do all that hopping and skipping around in those heels of yours. Aren’t you tired?”

    “Exhausted.” Lena billowed with a vapor, hand upon her brow like a Disney princess, “Wouldn’t you be surprised to know,” She teased, “Cinderella can do anything in heels.”

    “Weren’t they actually just glass slippers, though?”

    “That’s beside the point.”

    Kara began to bend down and scoop up one stack of baskets at a time, and Lena didn’t waste any time grabbing the ones ahead of her. Soon they were walking side by side, alternating baskets into their arms until they were all done and gathered. It was mostly dark now. The searing Texan sun had made its speedy retreat over the maple scattered horizon. They brought their teetering towers of baskets each through the wide bay door back at the winery and seemed to dust their hands at the same time, ready in unison to call it a day.

    There went Lena’s heart again, lurching forward as if to escape its rib cage of a prison once and for all. She scrambled for something —anything— to say at that point…

    “I’ve been meaning to ask- if it’s not too personal, of course—”

    “What’s up?” Kara offered with a smile.

    “The other morning when I went to see you at your home… That was your family?”

    “Hm.” Kara nodded, “That would be them.”

    “Have you… lived there long?”

    “About a year or so, I’d say. With my mom and my dad and my sister… let’s just say things got a little cramped after a while.” Kara went to lean against the tall rear tire of their harvester tractor. The machine itself had proven to be a great help whenever it came to traversing the wide and rolling hillocks of the vineyard. All thanks to the smiling foreman whose arm rested upon it.

    Lena had some difficulty taking her eyes off Kara again. She was simply the most attractive thing to fit inside a plain pair of blue jeans and boots. The light brown Shade Tree Cellars uniform shirts she wore always seemed to have their first few buttons undone, leaving space enough to display some very sun-kissed collarbones. Lena’s tongue literally felt as if it had tied itself into a knot… but Kara had seen fit to entertain the subject of their conversation, so allow her to continue she did.

    “My dad’s, um,” Kara ran her fingers through her hair, “He got sick a while back, and we’ve just been looking after him ever since. We lost the farm, but we’ll fight like hell to not lose him too.”

    “I’m sorry to hear that…”

    “It’s like each of us has a different idea of what’s best for him, and none of us can agree on any one thing.”

    “Is that why the accommodations for the position here caught your eye?” Lena asked as she came up and rested her own shoulder against the very tall tire beside Kara.

    “Honestly, yeah. I couldn’t take being stuck there anymore. I love my dad, but it’s just… a lot.”

    “I get it.”

    Kara turned her head at the sound of some of the villa cats squaring off with one another, and there upon the broad end of her jaw were the faint remnants of her bruise; The same bruise that Lena had seen just a few days earlier. It looked as though it had been quite painful at one point, and also like Kara had tried her best to cover it up with some tone-matching concealer… but there was only so much one could do with make-up in this kind of heat.

    Before Lena knew it, her hand had come up to take Kara’s chin into her hand. She pulled with a simultaneous step forward and noticed that Kara’s eyes had gone wide all of a sudden. Lena’s concern seemed to outweigh every other thought in her head at that point, the ones that screamed “no” and “stop” and “don’t do this”. The features of Kara’s face eventually turned soft and she stood still while Lena continued to inspect her closely.

    Was it any wonder that she cared? Kara was kind, and patient, and generous… and so, so wonderful to be around. Whoever had hurt her would have to be a complete and utter monster. Lena wanted to ask. It killed her for how badly she wanted to ask. But instead, she simply left her hand there to smooth a thumb back and forth over the yellowing remainder of Kara’s wound.

    It was an unspoken agreement between the two of them after that; to not talk anymore.

    Kara allowed Lena’s hand to cup very gently there at her jaw, and soon her eyes flitted heavily downward, staring straight onward to Lena’s lips. Breathless were the mere space of seconds that came next. Lena could feel herself swallow and then lean forward, drawn by some invisible force. Further, and further…

    “Lena!”

    Jack’s voice came from the catwalk at the front of the facility, and Kara and Lena jumped apart as if there had been a fire started between them. There was just enough time to busy their hands once again (with work) before Jack came gliding down the long metal staircase to meet them. He’d traveled there from behind many of the tall chutes which led underground, so it was impossible for him to have seen anything… Not that there had been anything to see.

    They hadn’t actually done anything.

    “There you are—” He said as he straightened his tie and then blustered to a stop, “You’re not ready.”

    “Ready?” Lena nearly whispered.

    “Our reservations!” He tapped at the expensive face of his wristwatch, “Come along, we’ll be late at this rate.”

    “Right! Sorry.” Lena apologized. She turned to look over her shoulder once Jack had begun to lead her away by the small of her back and saw that Kara was still standing there. “Goodnight…”

    Kara merely nodded in reply. Her eyes seemed to be trained upon Jack’s, whose dark irises stared back at her with a dagger-like intent. There seemed to be a message shared there, one which Lena had trouble deciphering. The short exchange between them struck her as very strange. Lena had been, all this time, under the impression that Jack didn’t know Kara from Eve.

    “What was that about?” She asked Jack while they made their way inside.

    “What was what about?”

    “Back there…”

    “Honestly, my darling. I don’t have a single clue as to what you’re talking about- But let’s hurry now, shall we? Wear that silky piece, the green one—” He gave a few gesturing flourishes with his hands, “You know the one I like.”

    Lena sighed once she left him to wait in the tall atrium. “Yes. Alright.”

    It should have bothered her more that she was still being led around in her thirties like a prized pony. Circumstance had always placed her at the receiving end of other’s wishes, yet never her own. The timing just never felt right. After boarding school, before college, after college… Would she be old and gray by the time she was able to muster enough courage to forge her own path? Or would she acquiesce for the rest of her life, right down to the clothes she wore?

    Chapter End Notes

    Wookiee31, I'm happy to give you this "wake" of buzzards as your next noun of assembly, or collective noun. It's one heard often where I live, since it refers to a more southern tradition of holding funerary wakes for loved ones. Though, I suspect "wakes" are held all over the place, are they not?
