    Lena’s heart began to pound a little further up towards her throat. It seemed to sit there right in the middle, blocking any attempt she made at swallowing. “As in, you’ll introduce me as your girlfriend?”

    “Is that something you want?” Kara asked, pulling them back through the door and out of the wind. It shut behind them with a solid metal -thunk-, and once again the world around them went quiet.

    “I,” Lena started to bluster her way towards the odd shape of words again, “I… Well, I mean, of course- I guess I just hadn’t expected—”

    “Would you wanna talk about it first, then?” Kara asked with another curious tilt of her head, “The ‘girlfriend’ thing, not the ‘meeting my parents’ thing. I sort of already told them we were coming…” There went that handsome grin across her face, right on cue. “Tomorrow, actually.”

    Finally, Lena was able to let loose a laugh. “Sure.” She replied, pecking one last kiss into Kara’s adorably red cheek. “On both counts.”

    Much too late on that first one though, Charming.

    The shoe definitely fits.

     

     

    Kara waved back from the carport; the smile on her face warm enough to melt the frozen ground. “Okay, mom… Bye!”

    “Bye, honey.” Mrs. Danvers called out from the front door of their trailer home, “Lena, sweetheart, thank you again for coming. We’ve enjoyed you. Come back and see us soon, okay?”

    “Yes ma’am. Thank you for a wonderful dinner!”

    Lena hadn’t quite understood the meaning behind a “Texan goodbye” until now. Where an Irish goodbye was slinking out the back without a word to anyone, a Texan goodbye was ever so much the opposite. It was long winded thanks and several hugs, forgetting parts of conversations that simply must be relayed at the very last minute, several more hugs, and then the repeating of thanks all before leaving the front drive. Half an hour, minimum.

    Kara nudged Lena with a bit more insistence so that she would climb up into the passenger’s seat of the Bronco. “At this rate we’ll be here until next week,” She said quietly from behind her, “Look- oh, now she’s about to go and grab her cookbook- Let’s go!”

    It wasn’t Lena’s fault that she and Eliza got along so swimmingly… The woman was a powerhouse, and just as much a rural genius as Kara was. Even though she might not have been the one to bring Kara into the world, it certainly was a blessing that she’d raised her all the same. Eliza Danvers was owed a debt of gratitude, because without her, Lena might never have known what a full heart could feel like.

    Halfway to begrudgingly, Lena situated herself inside the cab and fastened her seatbelt. A giggle escaped through her nose as she watched Kara race across the twin beams of their headlights. Once they were down the skinny blacktop and driving away from the trailer park, Lena leaned her head back against her seat and drank in the sight of the woman who sat beside her. Same tan Carhartt jacket, same dark wash blue jeans, same light blue sweater… She looked so good in blues, the sky ought to be jealous.

    Kara soon caught her staring and then reached a hand across to hang it upon the back of Lena’s seat. “Feel like goin’ home?” She asked, “Or maybe somewhere else?”

    Lena sighed contentedly, “Where did you have in mind?”

    “Nowhere, really. Maybe just a night drive, if you’d like?”

    “What- Like, just driving around?”

    “Sure,” Kara smiled, “We go slow, turn the music up, maybe find somewhere to park…” She shot Lena a wink with that last part.

    At first, Lena merely gave a playful scoff in reply. Was it any wonder that she wanted to skip straight to the end? Forget the aimless driving. She just wanted to be nearer to Kara. To feel her panted breaths across the front of her throat… the touch of her hands… the press of her lips… The backseat of the Bronco was roomy enough. She knew this because they’d spent several long hours there on occasion. Quite frankly, there were no two people in the whole of Marble Falls who were having more sex than they were. Lena was insatiable, so the decision was an easy one.

    “Okay.” She replied, pulling at the collar of her coat. Her legs went to tuck up underneath herself as she wriggled to sit sideways. The heat coming from the vents on the dash made her give out a sleepy yawn, but just the one. “Your mom is incredible, by the way. I don’t know if I’ve said that yet.”

    “You have.” Kara laughed. “About ten times now.”

    Lena gazed back at the dark road ahead of them. “I don’t know what it is… I think I’d like to just sit and listen to her talk, really. She’s so smart. How come you never mentioned she was a biochemist?”

    “Eh, it’s a bit of a sore subject in the house, actually. Not suitable conversation for the dinner table.”

    “Why is that?”

    Kara stretched a bit, one hand gripping the steering wheel at its twelve o’clock position. “She worked for an agriculture company for a long time that specialized in fertilizers. They muscled her out whenever she tried to blow the whistle on some possible groundwater contaminants. She’s been at home ever since.”

    “Oh.”

    “Dad was thrilled to have her around all the time, but we started to notice there was always just a little bit of sadness behind her eyes… The thing that fulfills her and keeps her happy now is taking care of us- which is why she’d have invited you back inside to look through all our photo albums if I hadn’t done something to get you outta there.”

    “What, are you afraid I might see your awkward childhood Halloween costumes?” Lena teased.

    Kara nodded animatedly, “Yes.”

    Lena threw her head back with a laugh. It was nice just to waste time with Kara like this, driving down one unpaved back road after another. This was what she liked the most about country roads, despite their horrific potholes. No light pollution. Nothing but the amber glow of the moon lit up the night’s sky, and even then, one might have difficulty finding their own hand in front of their face.

    The Bronco’s headlights tunneled deep through the trees in some places, and in others lit up the old brick of low railway bridges. Random graffiti painted the sides of dilapidated old barns, and some of the road signs they passed looked to have been peppered with bullet holes. Lena supposed that these were the subtle nuances of country living that Kara loved the most. She’d probably been looking at these same enormous white oak trees and railroad tracks for her entire life. Lena had never known what that could feel like; to be so deeply rooted to a place. It was that, as well as the loud affections from someone named “mom” that set her heart ablaze. Jeremiah was quiet, yet well-spoken nearly each and every time he opened his mouth, but he knew just to sit back and let Eliza have the run of the house whenever his “girls” where in it. Everything that poured out of them both was drenched with love, and that was what Lena was so fond of. She’d finally caught up to the nurturing that she missed out on during her childhood.

    After another little while of songs laced with steel guitar and the small sprinkling of late night rain, Kara turned them down a small drive and then through a wide open gate. More trees covered the road ahead of them like an eerily branched tunnel until they ended up next to a long stretch of field. Lena twisted around the other way in her seat and looked out the window once they came to a stop.

    “Where are we?” She asked.

    Kara had grinned from ear to ear in the window’s reflection. “No idea.”

    “…What?”

    “Yeah, I’d say we’re good and lost now.”

    “Lost?” Lena whipped her head around.

    “Yup.” Kara replied with a point towards the glovebox directly across from Lena’s knees, “Do me a favor and reach in there?”

    Lena unbuckled her seatbelt and leaned forward to pop open the compartment. A clear bottle with even clearer liquid immediately rolled out to greet her. She took it with a suspicious lift of her eyebrow and then screwed the cap off to smell its contents.

    That had been a mistake.

    Kara threw a hand out in caution, “Don’t—”

    But it was too late… Jesus Christ, did the inside of Lena’s nose burn.

    “Holy shit,” Lena threw her head back, “Is this what I think it is?”

    Kara seemed to puff out her chest a little bit, trying hard to humble herself at the same time. “It might be.”

    “Moonshine- are you for real?”

    “Raspberry moonshine, but yeah.”

    Lena suddenly felt bold; A welcome feeling whenever she was in Kara’s presence. She wanted to try again, but maybe this time with less of her nose and more of her mouth.

    “It’s strong…” Kara warned.

    “Yes, I got that from the very strong gasoline smell.”

    Kara’s eyebrows gave a funny waggle at that. “Raspberry gasoline.”

    Lena waited until she was done laughing to tilt the bottle to her lips. Going in was fine… Even the split second it took to wash over her tongue was fine… It was the absolute fire that she exhaled after swallowing that threw her for a complete loop. Lena sputtered and then coughed for a moment, and then she held a look of remorse from behind her hand— She was shocked, but also slightly pleased. It only took a few more seconds to find that distinctive raspberry aftertaste. Once Lena was done fussing over the way it had kicked her in the mouth, she realized the brew was actually quite palatable. After all the burning, of course.

    “Not bad.” Lena managed to squeak and then handed the bottle over to Kara.

    Kara couldn’t help but to laugh the entire time. “It’s alright?” She asked.

    “I guess if this was all there was during prohibition, then I suppose it’s alright.”

    “See, I knew you’d like it.” Kara teased. She took a swig and then waited for the burn to dissipate, “Sometimes I like to cut it with Sprite. Helps to put the fire out.”

    “Oh, is that what’s happening to my sinuses right now?”

    “I did warn you.”

    They laughed again, each fading into a long sigh. Kara reached down to find the lever to her seat back and leaned into it, making herself a bit more comfortable. “But I’m glad you met my folks… My dad’s been asking to have you over ever since his birthday.”

    “Aw.” Lena cooed, “Well, they’re wonderful. I’m only sorry it took me this long.”

    “Things are complicated.” Kara shrugged, “Don’t worry. They like you a lot.”

    “You think so?”

    “My mom would not have served dinner on the good plates if she wasn’t trying to impress you. That means she likes you. Trust me.”

    “That makes me happy.” Lena reached out for the bottle in Kara’s hands, but not before smoothing the backs of her knuckles softly across her cheek. “You make me happy.”

    Kara beamed with another smile; so much so that she could put the ethereal glow of the moon to shame. But then it fell from her face entirely, leaving a crestfallen, almost sullen expression in its place. She’d been doing that a lot, lately. Kara seemed so content one moment, and then the next like something infinitesimally worrisome tugged at the back of her mind. Lena had chalked it up to the delicate matter of where her bruises always came from, but for some reason it always smacked of something more. Like there was another gap that she was much more hesitant to bridge. Lena saw more evidence of it this time upon Kara’s face, although now it looked like she might actually come out and say it.

    “Can we, um—” Kara shifted some of the bulkier parts of her jacket around, needing something to do with her hands now, “Can we maybe talk about Jack?”

    Lena took a chance with another pull from the shine bottle. It was better the second time. More raspberry, less kick in the mouth. “Okay…” She said calmly, “What do you want to know?”

    “Did you love him?”

    All Lena heard was “Did I take you away from him?” Lena shook her head, “No. I mean, we’ve been in each other’s lives for so long… I do love him, but I was never in love with him. He’s one of my oldest friends. We’re better that way.”

    “Do you think you still would have married him? Or…”

    Again, there was that insinuating “Was it because of me?” that hung at the end of her question.

    Lena took her time, pondering good and hard in lieu of an answer. She didn’t wish to landmark her growing relationship with Kara on something that she may or may not have been responsible for. After a long moment of silence, she supposed that the truth would have to suffice.

    “I don’t think so.” Lena replied with a quirk of her chin, “I was unhappy, to say the least. You actually gave me back some of the courage that I had lost a long time ago. I think I still would have broken it off with Jack regardless of everything- but honestly, it might have taken longer without you.”

    Kara nodded. “So… what happened before?”

    “Before? What do you mean?”

    “You said you had lost your courage…?”

    “Oh.” Lena’s eyes went out towards the midnight fog that rolled across the field in front of them. She took another drink, smaller this time. All of her stress was leaving her body in droves thanks to its incredibly high-proof content. “Caught that, did you?” She sighed, “There was somebody else. When I was much younger, of course… and much, much more naïve.”

    “Let me guess, she did something to hurt you?

    Perceptive as ever.

    “Betrayed every sacred word we ever shared, more like. I went to boarding school, and did all the rebellious boarding school girl things- Fell in love, got my heart broken, shamed my family… The usual.”

    “Well.” Kara reached for the bottle, “Clearly she’s an asshole, and your family should be the opposite of ashamed.”

    Lena turned her head to adore the sight of Kara once again. It had always hurt to talk about her past… Until now. Lena wasn’t sure what to say apart from, “Thank you.”

    “But for the record,” Kara went on, “I’m glad she’s not around anymore, whoever she was. Less effort only having to steal you away from a man.”

    “Oh, please. You did not steal me away—” Lena squealed whenever Kara suddenly reached across the cab of the truck to pinch at her side. They fought playfully until their arms found themselves intertwined. Hands clasped, fingers laced. Eventually, their conversation turned away from the harsh feelings of Lena’s past and towards fonder memories instead; Things that Lena would never forget…

    The first time she’d laid eyes upon Kara. Hearing her laugh. Meeting her parents…

    “You do know how to get us back home, though… Right?” She asked.

    “Oh, one hundred percent- Absolutely.”

    “Kara.”

    “Okay, sort of. Generally speaking. Um, maybe.”

    Chapter End Notes

    Happy weekend!

    Hoo, doggy, I'm tired. Hope everyone had a good week. I'm guessing most of you are picking up on the switching of likes/dislikes by now. I'm pleased with the irony, seems a bit like kismet to me. Did you hear the ba-dum-tiss in the background whenever Lena mentioned Glee?

    So, the chapter title is another Irishism, and when translated back to its original Gaeilge looks something like this: "Níl aon tinteán mar do thinteán féin" It's basically the phrase "there's no place like home" but much prettier, like most Irish proverbs/sayings. Home's always been a place where you feel loved, in my experience- it's not so much about brick and mortar.

    Lastly, someone asked about a possible furry companion (for this story) last week, and I'm sorry to say that Kara and Lena will have to go on without one. I think I'm still working off the dregs of Caddo Lake Whoopsie Daisies in that aspect- but for future stories, how does everyone feel about Falconer!Kara...? A hawk would be cool, no? Rollin' that one around in the old mind palace, still. Crashing through the cobwebs. Good lord almighty, I need sleep. Goodnight! See you next week!

    *Kara and Lena shined responsibly in this chapter.

    Thanks for reading.

    Stones, Once Thrown

    Chapter Summary

    Winter arrives.

    Chapter Notes

    Finished a job early today, so I polished up another chapter. Here ya go.

    TW: violence, very brief blood

    Chapter Twelve: Stones, Once Thrown

     

    “That’s exactly what I was thinking!”

    Nia spread her hands apart where she stood in the middle of the wide hall that led to the atrium, gesturing in the place of something grand, and festive. She was excited for their Christmas holiday plans at the vineyard and had been constructing ideas for a party almost as soon as they had picked up the last paper cup from the success that was their Halloween bash.

    “Anything else?” Lena asked.

    Not one to miss a beat, Nia clapped her hands together with a twinkle in her eye, “And tinsel- tinsel everywhere.”

    “No tinsel.”

    “Okay, how about ornaments, then? Big, big ornaments!”

    “How big are we talking?”

    Nia stepped out of the way for a courier to drop something at the door of Lena’s office. They pushed a clipboard at her with papers to sign, which forced a gap in her excitable rambling only long enough for her to take up a pen and to swish her initials— and then she was back to party planning with lightning speed.

    “Huge.” She went on, “I’m talking obnoxiously big- something to hang from the A-frame at the front entrance just to make people go ‘wow, that’s a big Christmas tree ornament’.”

    Spoken like a true Texan…

    “Don’t you think we should have a big Christmas tree, then?”

    “Oh, the biggest!”

    Lena laughed as she waved at whoever was mopping the tile near the entryway. They waved back with an easy smile, and she was settled on it. They would “go big or go home” for the holidays. Lena knew it would be hard to convince Nia to scale down her vision —even in the slightest bit— so she decided that it would be much easier just to let her have the run of the mill, so to speak.

    “Alright. Run up a budget and get it to me by the end of the day.” Lena said with an affirmative nod, “…and hire a DJ this time. I love the local sound, but a Christmas party just isn’t a Christmas party without some truly tacky holiday hits.”

    Nia squeezed her hands into little fists and waved them excitedly in front of her face. “One order of Mariah Carey, comin’ right up!”

    “Oh, God- actually—”

    The sound of bootsteps echoed in from the end of the long hall all of a sudden, and Lena averted her attention there in an instant. She knew that hurried gait, and that familiar stomp. Kara was headed straight for them, but something about her countenance seemed so out of place… Lena wasn’t exactly sure what it was, but the butterflies that lived somewhere near her stomach (the ones that would usually burst into flight just at the sight of Kara) had all but run for the hills. An unusual reaction, to be sure. Something was definitely amiss…

    Kara’s sunbeam of a smile was nowhere to be found.

    She came to a stone-faced halt not quite halfway into the hall with a few pieces of paper clutched inside of her fist like a vice. It was like she’d hesitated at first; like something had stopped her in her tracks right before something else entirely took over.

    Before Lena could so much as ask, Kara powered forward again. The outrage in her voice bounded down the long hall like a barreling freight train, “You really did it, didn’t you?”

    She was angry. Shaking. Jaw clenched hard enough to give it that dull -pop- sound all the way from the back. Lena knew it well; she’d felt it for herself many a late night under the covers of Kara’s bed.

    Lena didn’t know what to say. She was caught completely off guard— never mind the fact that she’d never even considered whether or not Kara was even capable of anger. “What are you talking about?” She asked.

    “You just bought it. Just like that—” Kara snapped her fingers viciously, making Lena jump, “You knew I was still talking it over with my dad. I asked you to wait.”

    Something awful crawled up the length of Lena’s throat and washed the back of her tongue with dread. She immediately felt sick. “What- you mean the farm?” She blinked rapidly, still utterly confused, “Kara, I haven’t—”

    “Don’t even try to lie right now, your signature’s right here on the sale!”

    Kara thrust the papers in her hands then towards Lena, but Nia was the one to catch them first. She had moved quickly, using every bit of her small frame in a protective manner; Another unexpected trait from yet another person she cared for.

    What the hell was happening?

    “Settle down—” Nia began to say, but her eyes latched onto the papers with a surprised sort of look. She’d leapt at the chance to see whether or not it was true, and the sick feeling that thrust itself into Lena’s mouth had only grown more intense. Nia drew her gaze upwards again, and there in it Lena saw disappointment. Round, and wet, and pleading like an abandoned puppy.

    No.

    No no no…

    Nia huffed a hurt breath and shook her head as if to ask “why?”, but it was Kara’s booming voice to claim every last available inch of space inside Lena’s head again.

    “I went up to the bank just to try one last time to reason with them—” Kara seethed, “But they said that somebody had already made an offer far over value- and that they weren’t obligated to notify me, so they went ahead with it!”

    “Kara…” Lena held her hands out for the pages, needing to see for herself, “Listen to me, there’s been some sort of misunderst—”

    Once she saw it, she suddenly understood Kara’s wrathful ire.

    There it was.

    "L. Luthor"

    ...but it wasn’t her signature.

    Lena began to flounder for the nearest explanation, “I- wait a minute- I didn’t do this, you have to believe me.”

    Kara said nothing. She just fumed with red eyes and red cheeks, and looked more hurt than Lena had ever thought possible.

    “Kara, I didn’t sign this!” She insisted.

    “I’m wondering…” Kara shrugged the shoulders of her wool-lined jacket and continued bearing forward with a stare as cold as ice, “Whatever happened to ‘no more secrets’? Or did you just lie about everything?”

    “What? No, of course not!”

    Kara didn’t seem to hear her. Instead, she took a step forward. And then another. “I took you to my family’s farm and all you saw was dollar signs…” She shook her head into her hands then and gave one great stomp of her foot, making Lena jump again. “I’m so stupid! You weren’t even gonna tell me- My name isn’t anywhere on there.”

    “Let me explain,” Lena begged, “My family and I all have the same initials- I wouldn’t do this! Please, can we just talk in my office?”

    “There’s nothing left to talk about!” Kara shouted, barreling forward again, “It’s already done! It’s gone!”

    “Hey!” Nia barked, “Quit yelling at her.” She stepped directly into Kara’s path and went to walk her back by her arms, but it was futile. Kara was well rooted in her pain. The stubborn stump of her anger would not be pulled free.

    “Get off—” Kara shoved Nia away with a growl and then continued to advance towards Lena. “That’s what y’all come here to do… right?”

    “Kara, please…” Lena begged. For every step Kara took towards her, she took one back in equal measure.

    She knew that Kara’s hurt had long run away with itself. That much was plain to see. It attacked her from all sides: her father’s cancer, the loss of their family farm, and the rift caused therein by her cage fighting… Now, the sense of utter betrayal she felt was apparently enough to push her over the edge.

    “You just look at everything like its already yours and you don’t care who gets in your way, or who gets hurt—!”

    Nia tried stepping in front of her again. “I said quit it!”

    “You were right about one thing, though.” Kara hissed.

    Lena felt a sharp gasp pull through the space of her teeth whenever her back met the large glass wall of her office. She trembled with both of her hands held cautiously out in front of herself. “…Please.”

    Kara went to leer forward at her, “You said it yourself… All you do is take.”

    With that being said, Nia seemed to have enough of her older sister’s rampage. She shoved against her with all of her might and yelled now that her patience had been all but run dry. “Kara, stop it!”

    She could have yelled to her heart’s content, but placing her hands near Kara’s neck had been a mistake.

    On instinct, Kara took one step with her back foot and then shoved hard with her shoulder, forcing Nia to come flying off from her and then down towards the floor. The crown of Nia’s head met the tile there with a horrible -crack- sound, and Kara jolted instantly as if she herself had been the one to feel it. Her eyes were clear now, and her arms hung dejectedly at her sides. She was stunned.

    Nia lay there on the floor, folded limb over limb like a limp doll left in an uncaring, almost slapdash manner.

    She didn’t move.

    “Nia- Nia!” Lena ran to her at once.

    Hands and knees beside her on the tile now, Lena rolled Nia over and watched as her eyelids fought like hell to peel themselves open again. There was blood running from the godawful split at the crown of her head. Nia tried lifting a hand to feel around there, but it fell halfway up to her chest as if she were confused. She was conscious, at least.

    “Hey- it’s okay. You’re okay… Here, sit up,” Lena spoke softly as she helped Nia to reel herself upwards, “I’ve got you.”

    Gutted, Lena went to look over her shoulder and back at Kara.

    Kara merely stood there, frozen in place. She seemed to be stuck deep inside the hellish realization of what she’d actually done, regardless of her intentions or whether or not her body had acted completely on its own. Lena knew that she had only meant to shout, but Nia’s newfound protectiveness had apparently shone through at the wrong place and the wrong time. Kara hadn’t meant to. The truth of it was written all over her face…

    It said, “I’m a monster, I hurt the things I love.”

    “I’m sorry…” Kara said as she went to her knees. Her hands splayed flat against the tile and she hung her head between her shoulders, but only for a moment. When she looked back up, her eyes were rimmed red again. This time they glistened with remorse. The previous rage that had filled them up was no more. “Nia, I’m so sorry.”

    Nia blinked slowly, one eyelid lagging slowly behind the other. “What happened?” She asked, reaching up to touch the back of her head with a wince. Her eyes went big at the sight of blood marring the festive red and green paint of her nails.

    Kara fumbled for something else to say other than an apology— but it seemed that she came up short. In the place of words, she reached for Nia, who unexpectedly flinched. It was like kicking Kara right in the gut. She was devastated.  

    “Okay.” Lena muttered, feeling a rush of heat spread out from her collarbones. She was infuriated with Kara’s uncontrollable bullheadedness, and purely out of contempt, she said the first hurtful thing that came to mind…

    “Now I’m scared of you.”

    She knew her own anger backwards and forwards. She knew how messy things could become because of it. Her infrequent temper was her own problem to sort out, which was a thing that she’d been working on for years, but Kara’s seemed to be brand new. It fit her in an ungainly way, striking an untuned and shrill sort of chord within her. Kara’s eyes went sort of blank after that, almost as if she were staring longways into space and found herself lost to the vacuous, cold blue firmament of it. She eventually took herself to her feet and went to gesture that Nia allow her to pick her up, but Lena barred her arm across and only held Nia tighter.

    “I’ve got her.” She said, “Just… find my keys and pull my car around, please.”

    A quiet tear leapt from the end of Kara’s nose as she nodded, still sullen in her expression yet crying all the while. She turned the other way and went to fetch Lena’s keys. They would be spending a very silent drive all the way to the hospital after that, because Lena now shared one of Kara’s prior rage-filled sentiments…

    There was, indeed, nothing left to say.

    …for now, at least.

     

     

    —January—

     

    Lena stared out of her office window.

    The world outside seemed like such a hardened, wind-stricken wasteland. So flat, and brown, and yellow… The twiggy grapevines were bereft now of any signs of life, pulled back to just their woody stems and bare lattices…

    But the wind continued to howl, and so did her heart.

    Kara had been working the loading dock as the weeks scraped by, one after another. They hadn’t spoken a word to each other since Christmas, and now it was nearly the end of January. Lena missed her more and more with each passing day. She only wished that she could take it all back. Better yet, to erase it completely… But stones weren’t so easily un-thrown whenever there was still so much glass left to pick up. Little did Lena know how difficult it would be to get herself up each morning and to work like nothing had ever happened. Like her heart wasn’t completely torn from her chest. It was the hardest thing she had ever done; choosing to stay in a place where the source of her heartache was ever present.

    Their second vintage, thankfully, had turned out miles better than the first, and with the deal struck via their northern panhandle distributor, they had sales coming in from places as far as the Dakotas. After the initial shock of what had happened eventually wore off, Lena couldn’t help but to want to run to Kara and to pour herself into her arms, to breathe in some of her never-ending strength…

    …but Kara was continuously avoidant of her, at best.

    Some days whenever the loading bay doors came rolling down, Lena would be waiting in the eaves, hopeful of catching Kara so that they could talk, or at the very least look at one another… but Kara somehow managed to make herself scarce every time. She clipped into a jog whenever she heard Lena’s voice calling out for her, or shut herself up tight in a back room somewhere just to pretend to busy herself with more work. Sometimes she fled to the cellars down below, knowing their network of tunnels better than anyone else on the property.

    Lena wasn’t sure what hurt worse: having shared words with one another that they obviously didn’t mean, or not having the opportunity at all to even try to take them back.

    “Slow down, you’re not making any sense!”

    “How did she do it?! What did you say to Lillian, Sam?!”

    “About what—?! Holy shit, Lena, what is wrong with you? Wait- are you crying?”

    “She’s ruined everything- the fucking bitch! She went and figured out what would hurt me the most, and I didn’t even see it coming—!"

    It was equally as disappointing that she’d assumed her best friend back in New York had played a hand in any of it. Lena was ashamed of herself for days, having accused Sam of such a treacherous backstabbing.

    “Do you remember that blood pact we made when we were ten…? Before you left for boarding school? I don’t know about you, but I meant it on my end. I swore to never tell Lillian Luthor a thing for as long as I lived, and I’ve kept that promise. Now calm down, and tell me what’s happened.”

    Two weeks had gone by before Lena was able to bring herself to Kara’s front door. She’d laid a folder there with printed documents showing evidence of Luthor Holdings LLC having backed out of the sale of Kara’s family cactus farm, but even then, Kara still wouldn’t come anywhere near her.

    Much to Lena’s horror, it hadn’t been her mother’s signature on the sale, after all. It had been her father’s inked initials to seal the deal.

    Lionel had once again been made a victim of his wife’s poisonous plotting. Regrettably, this wasn’t the first time that he’d been used as a weapon towards his own daughter… Lena’s rather tumultuous history with her stepmother was laced with a number of these manipulative designs, though, this one had certainly hurt the most.

    All of her pleading with him to let her keep the vineyard was like moving the wrong piece further up the board. More so, Lena had been all too unaware that Lillian was playing a different game altogether. It burned deeply and at the very center of her soul; she’d gone this entire time not knowing what to be more afraid of losing. It was that goddamned rug again, pulled swiftly out from beneath her feet. It was maddening. How would she ever win?

    Lena still wished more than anything to tell Kara that she hadn’t meant what she’d said. She wasn’t afraid of her; that had only been her broken heart talking. She regretted it dearly.

    “It was all just a horrible mistake, Sam. I’d like to say that I can’t believe Lillian would actually do something like this, but the sad thing is, I can believe it. It’s just her style to be so calculating… God, this is all my fault. I shouldn’t have provoked her.”

    “Hey, listen to me- None of this is your fault. I’m just sorry that it took so long here on my end… I’m sure you can imagine the scowl I had to contend with whenever I told your dad that he’d been played. Again.”

    Sam had, of course, been putting it lightly. Lena only received the rarest of phone calls from her father whenever she and his frustratingly conniving wife had put him in the worst of moods. In his mind, women were still better seen and not heard, just as children were.

    “Yeah, I got an earful already, so I don’t actually have to imagine all that hard. What did you tell him, exactly?”

    “Well, I had to convince him that the place was a dump, basically. That it would cost him more in the end to try and get rid of it if he didn’t dissolve the sale. He must have lawyered his way out of it pretty quickly- I’ve never seen a deal reverse with that kind of speed. Did you get my fax?”

    “I did. Hey, Sam…? Thank you.”

    “No problem. So, how do you think she found out?”

    That was a question that still continued to plague Lena’s mind. She knew her mother to have claws with an almost endless reach to them. Lillian must have been paying pretty close attention to have discovered Lena’s true weakness…

    Which was, most definitely, Kara.

    “I don’t know… I think maybe she had somebody poking around the vineyard for a while. Will you call me tomorrow?”

    “Of course, babe, whatever you need. Is- is Kara still not talking to you?”

    Lena remembered the sharp sting of tears in her eyes every time Kara’s name came up…

    “It was just, so, so ugly… I’m sure she hates herself for it. We both said things we didn’t mean. If only she would just listen… I don’t know, Sam. You didn’t see how upset she was.”

    “You’re right, I can only imagine.”

    “I just miss her so much, it’s killing me.”

    “You just let me know if she doesn’t come to her senses soon. I’ll be on the first plane down there- I don’t care how many muscles you claim she has—”

    That had been the first time that Lena had laughed in weeks.

    “I’ll be fine. I love you, Sam.”

    “Okay. I love you too.”

    There were a few polite raps against her open door then that pulled her out of her day-long misery. It was good timing, if anything. The spinning of her office chair worked hard to bring her all the way back to the surface. Missing Kara was a lot like drowning.

    “Hey,” Jack leaned inside, “Brought you one of those sandwiches you like. I know you haven’t eaten.”

    Lena felt like dropping her head into her hands again. “Thanks. I’m not hungry.”

    “I’m aware, but I’m also not leaving until you take at least two bites.” Jack said as he tossed the wrapped sandwich down upon the large, flat calendar that lived atop her desk. There was a bright blue smiley face that was scribbled in marker on one side. Nia’s handywork, surely.

    Lena still harbored a fair bit of guilt for their maître d’ being caught in the crossfire… A clean bill of health had seen Nia back from the hospital quickly enough; just a few stitches and she was off like a shot again. There wasn’t much that slowed her down.

    “Seriously?” Lena asked, eyeing the sandwich. With a grimace, she realized just how grumpy she had sounded almost as soon as she had spoken.

    Instead of arguing or doubling down on her current disagreement with food (as it only made her sad like any other mindless, repetitive action) she went to unwrap it and laid it in front of herself, pausing in preparation of the required effort.

    Because that was what everything was.

    Goddamned effort.

    Getting up, getting around, getting dressed…. Even sleep was a thing of the past. It all required so much effort. Lena wasn’t even sure how she’d been able to make it from one day to the next. Being on autopilot for weeks on end was a specific kind of torture, to say the least.

    Nothing felt the same while Kara avoided every chance of an interaction with her. That, and the awful, grayish monochrome of winter didn’t help things either. Without Kara to brighten her days, everything was boring, pointless, monotonous… and in some cases, irritating.

    Jack ended up sitting in front of her desk in an effort to hold true to his claim. He stuck two fingers up and shrugged, “Two bites. Just eat and then go get some rest, yeah? Read a book, or something.”

    Chagrined, Lena reached for the sandwich but changed her mind at the very last second. So close…

    “I’ve tried that.” She replied with a heavy sigh instead.

    Even her favorite stories had failed to alleviate her suffering. One line could turn into two easily enough, but two almost never turned into three. Something as simple as a written word upon a page would remind her of Kara, and then her head would be spinning again. Music was just as awful, for some reason. Her despondent outlook on life had bled into everything she loved. It was a horrible place to be.

    “Okay. How about a vacation?”

    “I can’t leave the vineyard—” Lena suddenly stopped, having felt herself answer too quickly. “I mean… You might not think so, but there’s still quite a bit to do here. I have emails from—”

    Jack leaned forward and pushed Lena’s sandwich closer, cutting her off. She merely gave a great and awful huff right back at him for it. Lena knew he wouldn’t be satisfied until she ate something, so maybe she could at least struggle down one bite. The way her stomach ached on occasion was purely out of a need for sustenance. Damn the fool thing… Why wouldn’t it listen? Lena hated the thought of eating, because what was the point? To sustain a life she no longer found worth living? How foolish and naïve it made her feel, wanting to throw everything away over a woman…

    Over the only woman.

    “Fine.”

    Lena grabbed the grilled sandwich masterpiece and took a decent sized bite before she could talk herself out of it. Once the taste of grilled sourdough and swiss hit her tastebuds, her long lost appetite finally came throttling back, knocking at the door of her stomach like an unwelcome visitor.

    Maybe two or three bites couldn’t hurt…

    “Mm,” Lena chewed quietly behind her hand, “Thanks.”

    “Sure.” Jack tugged at the holes of his sleeves where his cufflinks used to sit and gave a few long stretches of his neck, “Now, tell me something… Are you okay?”

    Lena huffed a laugh between her second and third bites, “You know that I’m not, Jack.”

    “I just wish I’d known sooner- About Kara, I mean. If I had any idea what she meant to you—” He grinned apologetically as soon as Lena stared daggers at him from across the desk, “Erm- what she means to you, then maybe I could have been a little less, I don’t know…” Jack paused for a moment in search of the word that fit best, “Obtuse?”

    “I’m sorry.” Lena pushed the rest of her sandwich away and then cast her eyes downward again.

    “No, no—” Jack scooted towards the edge of his seat, reaching a hand over the desk, “Don’t apologize. In fact, I want you to stop doing that altogether.”

    “Apologizing?”

    “Yes.”

    Lena jawed silently in search of an objection, but the fact that she found none seemed to settle her nerves just a little bit. She took a deep breath in through her nose and then sat up straight. “Alright. I’ll try.”

    “Okay.” Jack said with a smile. “That’s all I can ask.”

    “Thank you…”

    “Of course.” He tipped his head after that with a nod. “But actually, could you do one more thing for me?”

    “Oh, my goodness- What?” Lena vexed playfully.

    “Come out with me tonight. Let’s grab a drink, for old time’s sake.”

    Lena went back to shaking her head, “I told you, I can’t—”

    “Can’t? Or won’t?”

    Lena ran her lips into a thin line with that. It seemed to mystify her how Jack could be so godawfully oblivious to some things, but still so mindful about others. He could probably sense Lena’s unwillingness to leave the vineyard, simply for the fact that it had nothing at all to do with “work”.

    Jack frowned sympathetically. “She’ll still be here when you get back.”

    Lena sighed. The thought of taking that long drive into the city only to return to an empty foreman’s quarters made her want to sob. She was afraid that Kara would take the opportunity to leave, but how much more worrying and crying could she do over something she had absolutely no control over? It pained her to remember a time when she thought that Kara would never hurt her. Lena spent almost every other moment alone unpacking that emotional landslide; whether or not that Kara had, in fact, hurt her… or if Lena had only hurt herself. Each and every time, she came to the same horrible conclusion.

    They had hurt each other.

    “Alright.” She agreed.

    She would go, but she certainly didn’t have to enjoy herself. If anything, maybe taking herself away from where the hurt slept would end up doing her some good— If only for a few hours, at least.

    Chapter End Notes

    *wears party hat, toots paper horn* I wrote my one millionth word in this chapter.

    Phew~ So, I've obviously written 'splodey Kara before, but never from Lena's full POV. Suffice it to say, it's not my favorite iteration of the character that I'm known to focus on, so I do believe that will be it for a while in the anger/outburst department. There are other avenues of human emotion to explore- some more than others I'd like to give a go in the future. For this story, I wanted to write something messy for once. I was looking for something a bit more like real life relationships (notice how I didn't say "healthy" relationships) and so there's some fallout to be had, of course... but keep the "happy ending" tag in mind, too.

    See you at the end of the week.
    Thanks for reading. <3

    It Takes a Little Mercy

    Chapter Summary

    Lena copes. (Barely).

    Chapter Notes

    CW: scary images, graphic depictions of death

    Chapter Thirteen: It Takes a Little Mercy

     

    —February—

     

    Where she’d found the courage to knock at Kara’s door finally, Lena had no earthly idea.

    Call it courage, or utter frustration, or even resentment… No matter what shade of hurt it was, she still found the side of her fist pounding there at the smooth wood of the familiar threshold. The same one that she had entered and exited from on so many nights and so many mornings. She was done with just letting it all go.

    She had to at least keep trying, didn’t she? Why couldn’t they be friends? Wasn’t how that how they had begun? Having the decision all but made for her certainly pissed her off more than anything. Lena knocked and knocked, but still, no one answered. Kara’s Bronco wasn’t parked in the small, gravel-lined drive, so she didn’t know why she even bothered…

    But bother she did, anyway.

    “Kara.” She laid her forehead against the door, “Please, if you’re in there…”

    “Wake up, beautiful.”

    The sound of Kara’s voice rooted Lena to the porch. She closed her eyes with a deep breath through her nose and did her best to remember.

    “Mm…” Lena whined from where she laid in Kara’s bed, “No. Come back to bed.”

    “Can’t. If that grass gets any taller out there, we’ll get lost in it.”

    “Fine by me.”

    “Come on, sleepyhead.”

    “Okay, but kiss me first?”

    She had always loved the fact that Kara could never get into bed like a normal human being. She either hopped, or bounced, or sometimes —if she were completely exhausted— fell face-first into it with a heavy -fwump- directly onto the quilt. Lena could still feel all the laughter she’d held in her chest.

    “I’ll see you tonight?”

    Kara kissed her nose. “Can’t wait.”

    With a small huff, Lena turned her back to the door and slid all the way down to sit with her knees to her chest there upon the porch. It felt like eons had passed since the last time she’d snuck there across the rolling hills of the vineyard. Winter had indeed taken her so very far away from that little spark of happiness she enjoyed. Why hadn’t she taken more time to cherish the small things?

    “Okay, what about Karaoke?”

    “Hm, every once in a while, I guess.”

    “Okay, that’s something!”

    “But only showtunes.”

    “So, you like musicals then?”

    “Eh.”

    “Oh, come on! You have to have at least one real hobby.”

    “…Does painting count?”

    “Yes, Kara. Painting counts.”

    Lena had only noticed the small art easel tucked away in a corner of Kara’s living room after so many weeks of sitting right next to it. She’d not thought to ask, “why do you have an art easel?” just like all the other things which she’d taken for granted. Lena’s nose began to run with the threat of tears again, and with a deep breath, she stood back up to quietly dust herself off. The sound of tires against gravel had her heart leaping into her throat in an instant.

    Much to her dismay, it wasn’t the Bronco. A truck pulled up along the drive just as a window came rolling down, and a man stuck his head out with a squint into the early morning sunshine.

    “Excuse me- can you tell me where receiving is?”

    Lena sighed with a short nod. “Yes, it’s this way. I’ll show you.”

     

     

    “Don’t you think so, Lena?”

    Lena blinked idly where she stared into space. She thought she’d heard her name, but her mind was a million miles away, as per usual.

    “Lena? Hey…”

    “What—” Lena finally shook her head and gave a small, invigorating roll of her shoulders to wake herself back up. She’d been thinking more about Eliza’s chocolate pecan pie and less about the smattering of cheese boards in front of her. “Oh, I’m sorry… What were you saying?”

    Jack narrowed his gaze towards her for a split-second, knowing all too well the recently absent state of her mind whenever it came to social niceties. He cleared his throat then back at the small table of guests and offered an ounce of humor there to offset the awkward silence. “We really work her too hard here…” The table chuckled quietly, and then Jack stepped to the side to usher Lena towards the other end of the wine bar.

    She wiggled away from him before they made it there, displeased with his hand at her back as if she were a child. “Stop, I’m fine—”

    “Clearly, you’re not.” His voice was firm. Immovable.

    “I—”

    “Why don’t you go and get some rest? Those dark circles beneath your eyes are really giving you away here.”

    Lena scoffed. “And this is why you’re still single, obviously.”

    “Would you just listen?”

    Lena shook her shoulders exasperatedly, “What do you want me to do? Just lay in bed all day?”

    “Lena,” Jack took her hands into his own, “I’ve got this. These people—” A small quirk of his chin gestured back towards the rather obnoxious party, “They just want to drink and talk about themselves for an entire evening. You know the type. Just take the night off.”

    “I’d rather be useful.”

    “If you call ‘useful’ staring at the same ceiling tile for half an hour like a mental patient, then sure.”

    “Now hang on—”

    “Just… go. Okay? Everything’s fine.” Jack shifted his weight then to adopt a softer stance, lowering his voice to speak a bit more delicately, “It’s been months, you know… This isn’t normal. People are worried about you.”

    Lena took her hands back with a swipe and then pursed her lips in the event that she might say something else she might regret. Jack had a way of annoying the piss out of her, sometimes. Such an overbearing and masculine trait, to dote so heavily upon her just because she was a woman—

    No.

    Stop.

    Lena hung her head with a small pinch at the bridge of her nose. Was she really still so out of sorts that her past traumas were returning with a vengeance? Jack doted so heavily upon her because he was her friend, for crying out loud. It had nothing to do with him being a man, or her being a woman. He was still deeply concerned for her, and all that did was make her sad. Big surprise, there.

    “I know.” She said, “I’ll handle it.”

    “Alright. Go take care of yourself.”

    “Yeah. I just…” Lena shrugged, “I don’t know what that looks like anymore.”

    “Then call me if you need me.”

    “I will. Goodnight.”

    The night air was balmy for nearly spring, and it clung to her skin almost as soon as she stepped outside. The small torches had been lit where they dotted a path away from the wine bar, and Lena followed them, head swimming and hands wringing together with all of her “what-ifs” and “shoulds” and “why can’ts”.

    What if things would never go back to “normal”? Had Kara left so much of a mark upon her life that it was now irrevocably changed?

    Should she keep trying to rebuild that broken-down bridge between them? Or would that just prolong the pain they both still felt?

    Why can’t I just let her go?

    Before long, the torch light from the very last lamp danced in front of her, and when Lena looked back up from her feet, there stood the entrance of the tall privet hedges. Bed was still such a terrible torture device, so what was an aimless walk and short time spent in her small, leafy corner of the maze? Her favorite chair and patio table inside called out to her like an old friend, and Lena took herself in without another thought. Left, right, a second right and then three more brought her to her old, familiar quiet place, but her chair was cocked out to the side as if someone had just been sitting there. The small dirt corner beneath the table that was bereft of any mulch glinted with a peculiar and somewhat metallic finish in the amber moonlight, and Lena bent down to take a closer look at it.

    Bottle caps…

    Bottle caps from a very specific brand of beer, no less.

    “Darlin’, I’ll drink anything with the word ‘light’ at the end of it. I’m not picky.”

    “But you like this brand the best?”

    “You could say that.”

    Lena was astounded, yet also slightly miffed. This was her spot.

    “Kara?” She spun around in place, looking to any of the possible exits within the maze, “…Kara!”

    It made no sense. Why would Kara come here of all places to drown her apparent sorrows? Was there still some part of her that longed for Lena? Damn it all if it didn’t give Lena just the slightest bit of hope… She didn’t know what to do with such a thing anymore. “Hope” was useless. It got her nowhere but hurt.

    So much for having a safe place to come and hang her head all by her lonesome. What were the chances that she’d catch Kara here again? Did she even want to after all this time? Lena felt that she was treading dangerous waters now, being the disgruntled ex-girlfriend slash boss that hounded the woman she still loved both day and night. Every other thought she had was “leave her alone” or something to that same effect, but it did little to disarm her heart where it constantly overruled her brain. Torn asunder was every last little bit of control she once had over her feelings. Her phone began to ring all of the sudden in the front pocket of her burgundy slacks, and Lena didn’t even waste a second towards seeing who it was. She simply left it face down upon the skinny metal mesh of the table and turned on her heels to leave.

    She didn’t care anymore.

    She couldn’t afford to.

     

     

    Since when was it so dark down here in the cellars?

    Lena reached up to give a small -tink- against the brittle glass of the nearest hanging lamp. It flickered intermittently before popping on with its dull, illuminating light. The string of them worked poorly to drench the stonework archway in front of her with a lazy, almost bronze sort of color. Hazy were the edges around it, as if the concrete walls on either side undulated to and fro. Lena stared a little longer, and a hard fistful of fear leapt upwards from her gut.

    She clapped her hand over her mouth in an instant, hesitant to volunteer any more precious oxygen— but she blinked slowly and pulled in a deep breath through her nose just to check. The air was fine; not muddied with carbon dioxide. Being so deep underground, it was common to become short of breath if the work ran a bit long. Lena blinked until the walls in front of her eventually became still.

    Wait…

    When had she even come downstairs?

    Lena spun around on a dime and looked towards the long tunnel ahead of her. There she could see more of the hanging lamp lights still flickering like the one she’d just touched. They were wired along the ceiling with long, draping stretches of cord, maybe every ten to fifteen feet between the tall, load bearing timbers. Lena saw a number of their wine barrels in a nearby alcove settled upon their sides. They laid nice and neat there in their corrals, just as they were meant to. She walked over and ran her fingertips across the iron ribs of each, wondering just how she had come to be down in the cellars when she thought she’d gone back to her room back at the villa instead. It came as no surprise that she’d probably daydreamed herself there… Lena had been finding herself in the strangest of places, lately. Her wandering feet would surely mean her doom, should she run afoul of the grape crusher or some other piece of heavy machinery without paying attention.

    Purely out of nowhere, a booted -clop- came from the other end of the tunnel, and then the long, dragging -squeak- of a door followed.

    “Hello…?” Lena spoke up. Her voice traveled all the way down to the next archway with its large riverbed stones and cinderblock outfit, but the sound of her greeting made no attempt to travel back like a true echo would. It just went on, and on, and on…

    The small, struggling light that hung above her head suddenly flickered all the way out, and Lena was instantly plunged into darkness. The dry, almost hollow sort of cold that swallowed her up was like standing at the bottom of a freshly dug grave.

    How in the hell did she even know what that felt like?

    Something inside of her begged to run, and so she did. Frantic with both hands held out towards the only available light in front of her, the end of the tunnel seemed to draw further away the more she ran. The cellar shaft stretched in spite of her, but she at least made it beneath the next hanging lamp, panting with all of her frightened effort. The lamp’s pitiful light choked in and out just like the first one had, and Lena’s hand went there without thinking to give it another gentle tap. Somehow, it looked exactly the same. Her hand, her curiously outstretched finger, as well as the little -tink- sound the polished end of her nail made against the glass… Burst forth with light now, it washed a nearly identical stone archway in front of her with the same eerie bronze color. Lena finally realized that she’d not moved an inch. She was still standing in the same place where she’d begun.

    The booted -clop- and -squeak- of the far away door came rolling down the tunnel again, just as it had the first time.

    “Hello!” She called out again.

    The sound of a pained cough came from behind her all of the sudden, and Lena turned to see Kara standing there, struggling to stay upright and on her feet. Her hands were at her throat, and she was sputtering for air.

    “Kara…” Lena went to her quickly, “Kara- what’s wrong?”

    It appeared as though that Kara was unable to speak. Her lips were devoid of their wonderful pink hue and were instead rimmed with blue, and she tore at the collar of her brown work shirt like she couldn’t breathe. Try as she might, her fingertips couldn’t quite wrap around the button there at her collar. She was choking. The way Lena’s heart slammed violently against the backs of her ribs painted a very clear picture; Nothing had ever scared her more.

    “Hey, look at me—” Lena begged as she swatted Kara’s hands away from her neck. She couldn’t get the incessant button to thread through its small hole either, so she took the fabric of Kara’s shirt in both of her fists and pulled, forgoing the buttons altogether. Even with her shirt ripped clean down its middle and her neck now completely free of constraint, Kara was still unable to take in a single breath. Her eyelids fluttered helplessly, and Lena did her best to catch her on their way down to the cold, hard ground. “Breathe, Kara!” She shouted.

    Remembering the masks with their small oxygen canisters attached —the same ones that were normally hung in this room in case of emergency— Lena swiveled her head around left and then right in search of them… but found each of the hooks to be empty.

    “Kara, you have to breathe, listen to me…” Lena tried pushing what terrified breath of her own she still possessed directly into Kara’s mouth, but it seemed to do nothing.

    Kara’s magnificent blue eyes focused on her, wide open and nary a blink to them, and then, after a few more moments of frightened fidgeting and clawing at her neck, she fell still. There were no more awful, ragged gasps for air, and no more fretful kicking of her heels into the ground…

    She was just gone.

    “Hey…” Lena shook one of her shoulders hard, insisting that she move again, or at the very least, blink. “Kara?”

    Any previous signs of life that had once gleamed inside of Kara’s eyes were no more. There was nothing left behind them, leaving their baby blue color slightly grayed now. There was but an emptiness there, and a slack expression of sheer terror stuck forever upon her face.

    “No!” Lena screamed, “No! No—”

    “No—!”

    Lena thrust herself forward in her bed, chest heaving with the horrific weight of her nightmare. A chill quickly set upon her skin as she threw her legs out from beneath her fluffy duvet. The magnitude of her dreams were becoming more and more with each passing night, but she’d not dreamt of Kara’s death until now. Before, it was just the haunting images and scenarios of Kara being there one minute and then gone the next. Lena would witness her from afar, somewhere out in the fields on a fine, sunny day, and then she would suddenly blink out of existence entirely as if she had never even been there at all. The loss Lena felt deep in the marrow of her bones had now found its way into her nightmares…

    …but watching Kara die was so far beyond cruel.

    What had she done to deserve such persecution?

    With her silk nightrobe in hand, Lena slipped it quickly over her shoulders as she ran from her bedroom at the far end of the villa just to make it to the top of the next hill. There in the low hanging moonlight she could see Kara’s truck parked right in its drive, right where it belonged. To make a mad dash there just to knock at her door would be foolish, she supposed. What sort of stereotypical behavior would that look like? Was she completely mad now with grief? Was she that ex? It was unfair even to consider; they had barely even arrived at such a status to begin with.

    Lena had brushed her fingertips right up against the feathery beginnings of true love, as if to stroke softly at the heavenly ends of angel’s wings, just to have them torn away again.

    She was no stranger to feeling as though she’d been left all alone in the world. So why did it terrify her so much this time? What had Kara done to her?

    The bitter indignation she tried and failed to rid herself of came laughing all the way back from the jagged recesses of her broken heart. She’d make herself sick again with the emotional whiplash of it, flinging helplessly back and forth between sadness and fury. Lena knew that she would land somewhere eventually.

    She just had to pray that it wouldn’t change her completely.

    Chapter End Notes

    "Alexa, play Blue Hour by Anna Graves."

    Our poor girl. She's hangin' in there.
    Thanks for reading.

    A Drawbridge, Lowered

    Chapter Summary

    Spring arrives. Lena discovers her limits.

    Chapter Notes

    CW: brief suicidal ideations

    Chapter Fourteen: A Drawbridge, Lowered

     

    —March—

     

    Jack’s text message had said to come quickly.

    Whatever was so damn important all the way at the top of the belltower apparently couldn’t wait. Lena watched the rubbed-raw spot on her left converse as it traveled up and down each step, groaning with the effort it took to make one flight of stairs after another. There were plenty more to climb, and Lena was not in the mood.

    She’d had quite enough of him lately, Jack and his never-ending umbrella of concern.

    Spend too long on her feet and it was “off to bed, you.” Break so much as a single drop of sweat and it was “find some shade, yeah?” He cared, yes, but maybe too much. Lena was beginning to feel like a porcelain doll left to sit idly upon a shelf— lean too far this way and she could crack her very fine outer shell, or tip over that way and end up ruffling all of her pretty frills and lacework hems. Lena swallowed down the urge to shout at him every time, “I’m not going to break, Jack!”
